\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(\alpha q)dq\Bigg|\leq ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{12}.

To do so, we apply
Lemma

3.17
and
Lemma

3.20
to compare the Toda lattice
\bm{q}(s)
at time
s
to a Toda lattice at thermal equilibrium, and then use
Proposition

4.5
.

So, set
K=T(\log N)^{9/2}
, and observe that
j\in\llbracket N_{1}+5K,N_{2}-5K\rrbracket
. Then
Lemma

3.17
yields a random matrix
\tilde{\bm{L}}=[\tilde{L}_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
with the same law as
\bm{L}(0)
, and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which

(4.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket}|L_{ii^{\prime}}(s)-\tilde{L}_{ii^{\prime}}|\leq e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{4}}.

We restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
in what follows. Analogously to in
Definition

1.9
, define the Flaschka variables
\tilde{\bm{a}}=(\tilde{a}_{N_{1}},\tilde{a}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{a}_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N-1}
and
\tilde{\bm{b}}=(\tilde{b}_{N_{1}},\tilde{b}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
associated with
\tilde{\bm{L}}
by setting
\tilde{a}_{i}=\tilde{L}_{i,i+1}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
; setting
\tilde{a}_{N_{2}}=0
; and setting
\tilde{b}_{i}=\tilde{L}_{i,i}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Let
(\tilde{\bm{p}};\tilde{\bm{q}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
denote the Toda state space initial data associated with
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
(as described in
Section

1.2
), and denote
\tilde{\bm{p}}=(\tilde{p}_{N_{1}},\tilde{p}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{p}_{N_{2}})
and
\tilde{\bm{q}}=(\tilde{q}_{N_{1}},\tilde{q}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{q}_{N_{2}})
. Set
\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}=(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\tilde{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{N})
; let
\tilde{\varphi}:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote a
\zeta
-localization center bijection for
\tilde{\bm{L}}
; and denote
\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}=\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{\varphi}^{-1}(i)}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
.

By
Proposition

4.5
there exists an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, on which

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(\alpha q)dq\Bigg|\leq 7ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{11}.

We further restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
in what follows. To verify (
4.10
), it therefore suffices to show with overwhelming probability that

(4.12)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|<ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{11}.

To that end, we define three additional events on which we will be able to compare
\bm{L}(s)
and
\tilde{\bm{L}}
. The first is that on which
\bm{L}(s)
and
\tilde{\bm{L}}
are bounded in a particular way, namely,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{3}=\mathsf{BND}_{\tilde{\bm{L}}}(\log N)\cap\bigcap_{r\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(r)}(\log N)\cap\bigcap_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}\{\tilde{L}_{i,i+1}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\}.

Observe that
\mathsf{E}_{3}
is overwhelmingly probable, where the probability estimate on the first two events in (
4.13
) follows from
Lemma

3.13
, and that on the third event in (
4.13
) follows from the explicit form (
Definition

1.1
) for the thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;N-1,N}
.

To define the second event, observe by (
4.11
) and
Lemma

3.20
that there exists an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, on which the following holds. There exists a bijection
\psi:\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
such that, for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+2K,N_{2}-2K\rrbracket
, we have

(4.14)

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{i}-\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)}|<c_{4}e^{-c_{4}(\log N)^{3}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\psi(i)-i|\leq(\log N)^{2}.

The third event is that on which the
\tilde{q}_{i}
are separated as indicated by
Lemma

3.10
; specifically, let

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{5}=\bigcap_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}

\displaystyle\{|q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)-\alpha(i-j)|<|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\}

\displaystyle\quad\cap\{|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}-\alpha(i-j)|<|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\}.

By
Lemma

3.10
and a union bound,
\mathsf{E}_{5}
is overwhelmingly probable. Thus, setting
\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{5}\mathsf{E}_{i}
, it suffices to verify (
4.12
) upon restricting to
\mathsf{E}
. So, we restrict to
\mathsf{E}
in what follows.

First observe by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
that for
|i-j|\geq K\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}
we have
|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|>3S
and hence
G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})=0
by the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
. Moreover, by (
4.11
); our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, with the third event in (
4.13
); and (
1.9
), we have for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}+K\rrbracket
that

(4.15)

\displaystyle|(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})-(q_{i}-q_{j})|\leq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}|\log a_{k}-\log\tilde{a}_{k}|=2\displaystyle\sum_{k=i}^{j-1}|\log L_{k,k+1}(s)-\log\tilde{L}_{k,k+1}|\leq e^{-c_{6}(\log N)^{3}},

for some constant
c_{6}>0
. In particular, it follows since
j\in\llbracket N_{1}+5K,N_{2}-5K\rrbracket
that for
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
with
|i-j|\geq K
we have
|q_{i}-q_{j}|\geq|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|-e^{-c_{6}(\log N)^{3}}>2S
. Moreover, for
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle|q_{i}-q_{j}|

\displaystyle\geq|q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)|-|q_{i}(s)-q_{i}(0)|-|q_{j}(s)-q_{j}(0)|

\displaystyle\geq|q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)|-2T\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}}\big(|L_{ii}(s)|+|L_{jj}(s)|\big)

\displaystyle\geq|q_{i}(0)-q_{j}(0)|-2T\log N\geq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot|i-j|-\displaystyle\frac{K}{\log N}>2S,

where in the first and second statements we used (
1.5
) and the fact that each
p_{k}(s)=b_{k}(s)=L_{kk}(s)
by (
1.7
) and
Definition

1.9
; in the third we used our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
; and in the fourth and fifth we used our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
and the definition of
K
.

Hence, for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|i-j|\geq K
, we have
|q_{i}-q_{j}|>2S
and thus
G(q_{i}-q_{j})=0
(again by the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
). Therefore,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}&\Big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\Big|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i=j-K}^{j+K}\big(|F(\Lambda_{i})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})|\cdot|G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})|+|F(\Lambda_{i})|\cdot|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})|\big)\\
&\leq 2BN\cdot Ae^{-(\log N)^{2}}+A\displaystyle\sum_{i=j-K}^{j+K}\big|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|.\end{aligned}

Here, in the first bound, we used the above fact that
G(q_{i}-q_{j})=G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})=0
whenever
|i-j|\geq K
. In the second, we used the facts that
|G(q)|\leq 2B
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
(by the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
); that
|F(\Lambda_{i})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
by the third part of
Assumption

4.1
, the fact that
|\Lambda_{i}|,|\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}|\leq\log N
by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
, and (
4.14
); and the fact that
|F(\Lambda_{i})|\leq A
by the second part of
Assumption

4.1
(and again our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
).

Next observe from (
4.15
) and the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
that

(4.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sum_{i=j-K}^{j+K}\big|G(&q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\\
&\leq BS^{-1}N\cdot e^{-c_{6}(\log N)^{3}}+B\displaystyle\sum_{i=j-K}^{j+K}(\mathbbm{1}_{q_{i}-q_{j}\geq 0\geq\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}\geq 0\geq q_{i}-q_{j}}).\end{aligned}

Due to (
4.15
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
, if
|i-j|\geq(\log N)^{5}
, both
q_{i}-q_{j}
and
\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}
are nonzero and have the same sign as
\alpha(i-j)
(since they are both within
2|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}<|\alpha i-\alpha j|
of
\alpha(i-j)
). Together with (
4.17
), this gives

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=j-K}^{j+K}\big|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\leq BN^{-1}+B\cdot 4(\log N)^{5}\leq 5B(\log N)^{5}.

Upon insertion into (
4.16
), this implies (
4.12
) and thus the proposition. ∎

5.
Proofs of
Lemma

4.6
and
Lemma

4.7

In this section we establish
Lemma

4.6
and
Lemma

4.7
. We begin in
Section

5.1
by stating a general concentration bound (
Lemma

5.2
), similar to the McDiarmid inequality, that bounds the fluctuations of multivariate function in terms of how much each of its arguments “influences” it. We then prove
Lemma

4.6
assuming certain influence bounds given by
Lemma

5.3
, which are shown in
Section

5.3
. We next show
Lemma

4.7
in
Section

5.4
.

5.1.
Concentration Estimates

In this section we state a concentration bound, which is similar to the McDiarmid inequality, that we will use to show
Lemma

4.6
. To that end, the following definition provides the notion of how a random variable “influences” a multivariate function.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an index set, let
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}}
be a sequence of mutually independent real random variables, and let
F:\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{I}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function. For nonempty subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, define the set of variables
\bm{x}(\mathcal{J})=(x_{j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
. Then, for any real number
p\geq 0
, define the
influence

\Infl_{\bm{x}(\mathcal{J})}(F;p)=\Infl_{\bm{x}(\mathcal{J})}(F;p;\bm{x})
of
\bm{x}(\mathcal{J})
on
F
by

\displaystyle\Infl_{\bm{x}(\mathcal{J})}(F;p)=\displaystyle\inf\{A\geq 0:\mathbb{P}[|F(\bm{y})-F(\bm{x})|\geq A]\leq p\}.

Here, the sequence
\bm{y}=(y_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{I}}
is a family of mutually independent random variables, obtained by setting
y_{j}=x_{j}
if
j\neq\mathcal{J}
, and setting
y_{j}
to be a random variable with the same law as
x_{j}
that is independent from
\bm{x}
if
j\in\mathcal{J}
.

The next lemma is a variant of the McDiarmid inequality providing a concentration result for functions of random variables, in terms of their influences; it is in a similar direction as, but slightly different from,
[
11
, Proposition 2]
and
[
44
, Theorem 1.2]
. Its proof is given in
Appendix

C
below.

Lemma 5.2
.

Adopt the notation of
Definition

5.1
. Let
m\geq 1
be an integer and
\mathcal{J}_{1}\cup\mathcal{J}_{2}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{J}_{m}=\mathcal{I}
be a partition of
\mathcal{I}
into
m
disjoint, nonempty subsets. Denote

(5.1)

\displaystyle\qquad S=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Infl_{\bm{x}(\mathcal{J}_{k})}(F;p)^{2};\qquad U=\mathbb{E}[F(\bm{x})^{2}]^{1/2}.

Then, for any real number
R\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\big|F(\bm{x})-\mathbb{E}[F(\bm{x})]\big|\geq RS^{1/2}+2m^{1/2}p^{1/4}U\big]<2mp^{1/2}+2e^{-R^{2}/4}.

5.2.
Proof of
Lemma

4.6

In this section we establish
Lemma

4.6
, adopting the notation of that proposition throughout. In the below, we abbreviate the Flaschka variables
\bm{a}(0)=\bm{a}=(a_{N_{1}},a_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{N_{2}-1})
and
\bm{b}(0)=\bm{b}=(b_{N_{1}},b_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,b_{N_{2}})
. Define the function
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{N-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})=\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j}),

where we observe that
\mathfrak{F}
can indeed be viewed as a function of the random variables
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
, since
\bm{L}
and the
(q_{j})
can be. Next, define the variable sets (recalling
j\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}\rrbracket
is fixed)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\{a_{i},b_{i}:|i-j|>S(\log N)^{9/2}\},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}_{k}=(a_{k},b_{k}),

for any integer
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|k-j|\leq S(\log N)^{9/2}
. Recalling the notation from
Definition

5.1
, abbreviate for any such
k
the influences

\displaystyle I=\Infl_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathfrak{F};\mathfrak{p};\bm{a}\cup\bm{b}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad I_{k}=\Infl_{\mathcal{S}_{k}}(\mathfrak{F};\mathfrak{p};\bm{a}\cup\bm{b}),\qquad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{p}=e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}},

for some sufficiently small constant
\mathfrak{c}>0
that we will fix later. We will deduce
Lemma

4.6
from
Lemma

5.2
and the following lemma bounding these influences, which we establish in
Section

5.3
below.

Lemma 5.3
.

The following hold if
N\geq 1
is sufficiently large and
\mathfrak{c}>0
is sufficiently small.

(1)

For each
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
|k-j|\leq S(\log N)^{9/2}
, we have
I_{k}\leq AB(\log N)^{5}
.

(2)

We have
I\leq AB(\log N)^{5}
.

Before proving
Lemma

4.6
, we require the following quick lemma bounding the expectation of the maximal value of
|F(\lambda)|
over
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix
v>0
. For
N
sufficiently large, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|F(\lambda)|^{v}\bigg]\leq 2A^{v}.

Proof.

Recalling
Definition

3.12
, define the event
\mathsf{E}_{0}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
. Then, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|F(\lambda)|^{v}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq A^{v}+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}A^{v}e^{v|\lambda|^{1/2}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{0}^{\complement}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq A^{v}+A^{v}\displaystyle\int_{\log N}^{\infty}e^{v|x|^{1/2}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|\lambda|\geq x\bigg]dx

\displaystyle\leq A^{v}+c^{-1}A^{v}N\displaystyle\int_{\log N}^{\infty}e^{v|x|^{1/2}-cx^{2}}dx\leq 2A^{v},

where in the first bound we used the first and second statements in
Assumption

4.1
; in the second we used the definition of
\mathsf{E}_{0}
; in the third we used
Lemma

3.13
; and in the fourth we used that
N
is sufficiently large.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.6
.

This will follow from
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
. Set
U=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})^{2}]^{1/2}
. Then applying
Lemma

5.2
, with the
(R,p)
there equal to
(\log N,\mathfrak{p})
here and the
\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\bm{x}(\mathcal{J}_{k})
here equal to
\mathcal{S}\cup\bigcup_{k:|k-j|\leq S(\log N)^{9/2}}\mathcal{S}_{k}
here, we obtain

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\big|\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})-\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})]\big|\geq ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{15/2}+2N^{1/2}\mathfrak{p}^{1/4}U\big]\leq 2N\mathfrak{p}^{1/4}+2e^{-(\log N)^{2}},

where we used the fact from
Lemma

5.3
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=-\lfloor S(\log N)^{9/2}\rfloor}^{\lfloor S(\log N)^{9/2}\rfloor}\Infl_{\mathcal{S}_{k}}(\mathfrak{F};\mathfrak{p})^{2}+\Infl_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathfrak{F};\mathfrak{p})^{2}\leq 3S(\log N)^{9/2}\cdot(AB)^{2}(\log N)^{10}\leq A^{2}B^{2}S(\log N)^{15}.

Thus, it suffices to show that
U\leq 4ABN
for sufficiently large
N
, as then insertion into (
5.3
) (and using the definition of
\mathfrak{p}=e^{-\mathfrak{c}(\log N)^{2}}
) would yield the lemma. Since
|G(q)|\leq 2B
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
(by the fourth statement in (
4.1
)), this follows from (the
v=1
case of)
Lemma

5.4
. ∎

5.3.
Proof of
Lemma

5.3

In this section we establish
Lemma

5.3
; we adopt the notation of
Section

5.2
throughout.

To address both parts of
Lemma

5.3
simultaneously, we fix an index
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
with
|k-j|\leq S(\log N)^{9/2}
, and define the subset
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
by either setting
\mathcal{D}=\{k\}
or setting
\mathcal{D}=\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:|i-j|>S(\log N)^{9/2}\}
; in the first case we set
\mathfrak{I}=I_{k}
, and in the second we set
\mathfrak{I}=I
. We must estimate
\mathfrak{I}
, to which end we set notation for replacing the random variable
a_{i}\in\bm{a}
and
b_{i}\in\bm{b}
with independent copies of them, whenever
i\in\mathcal{D}
.

For each such
i\in\mathcal{D}
, let
a_{i}^{\prime}
and
b_{i}^{\prime}
be mutually independent random variables with the same laws as
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
, respectively, that are independent from
\bm{a}\cup\bm{b}
. Define
\tilde{\bm{a}}=(\tilde{a}_{N_{1}},\tilde{a}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{a}_{N_{2}-1})
and
\tilde{\bm{b}}=(\tilde{b}_{N_{1}},\tilde{b}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{b}_{N_{2}})
by setting
\tilde{a}_{i}=a_{i}
and
\tilde{b}_{i}=b_{i}
if
i\notin\mathcal{D}
, and by setting
\tilde{a}_{i}=a_{i}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{b}_{i}=b_{i}^{\prime}
if
i\in\mathcal{D}
. Then, by
Definition

5.1
,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}=\inf\big\{A\geq 0:\mathbb{P}\big[|\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})-\mathfrak{F}(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})|\geq A\big]\leq\mathfrak{p}\big\}.

Let us set some additional notation parallel to that in
Assumption

1.12
for
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
. Let
\tilde{\bm{L}}=[\tilde{L}_{ii^{\prime}}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
denote the tridiagonal matrix associated with
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
, as in
Definition

1.9
(so
\tilde{L}_{i,i+1}=\tilde{L}_{i+1,i}=\tilde{a}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
and
\tilde{L}_{i,i}=\tilde{b}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
); in this way, we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{ii^{\prime}}=L_{ii^{\prime}},\qquad\text{if either $\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2$ or $\dist(i^{\prime},\mathcal{D})\geq 2$}.

Set
\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}=(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\tilde{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{N})
; let
\tilde{\varphi}:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
denote an arbitrary
\zeta
-localization center bijection for
\tilde{\bm{L}}
; and set
\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}=\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{\varphi}^{-1}(i)}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Let
(\tilde{\bm{p}};\tilde{\bm{q}})
denote the Toda state space initial data associated with the Flaschka variables
(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})
, as in
Section

1.2
. Set
\tilde{\bm{q}}=(\tilde{q}_{N_{1}},\tilde{q}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\tilde{q}_{N_{2}})
.

By (
5.4
), to estimate
\mathfrak{I}
we must bound
|\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})-\mathfrak{F}(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})|
with high probability; we next introduce several events on which such a bound will hold. The first and second are those on which
\bm{a}
,
\eig\bm{L}
,
\tilde{\bm{a}}
, and
\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}
are bounded. Recalling
Definition

3.12
, set

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{1}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)\cap\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{\tilde{L}}}(\log N);\quad\mathsf{E}_{2}=\bigcap_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\{a_{k}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\}\cap\{\tilde{a}_{k}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\}.

Then
\mathsf{E}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable by
Lemma

3.13
, and
\mathsf{E}_{2}
is overwhelmingly probable by the explicit densities of
a_{k}
and
\tilde{a}_{k}
(from
Definition

1.1
).

The third event is that on which
\eig\bm{L}
and
\eig\tilde{\bm{L}}
are close to each other. Specifically, by
Lemma

3.20
(using (
5.5
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
to verify its hypothesis (
3.7
) at
\delta=0
), there is a constant
c_{1}>0
and an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
, on which the following holds. There exists a bijection
\psi:\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\rightarrow\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
such that, for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with either
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq(\log N)^{3}+2
or
\dist(\psi(i),\mathcal{D})\geq(\log N)^{3}+2
, we have

(5.6)

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{i}-\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)}|\leq e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{3}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\psi(i)-i|\leq(\log N)^{2}.

The fourth event is that on which consecutive
q_{i}
and
\tilde{q}_{i}
are not too close or far, namely,

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{4}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\\
i-i^{\prime}\geq(\log N)^{2}\end{subarray}}\bigg\{\big|q_{i}-q_{i^{\prime}}-\alpha(i-i^{\prime})\big|+\big|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}-\alpha(i-i^{\prime})\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot(i-i^{\prime})\bigg\}.

By
Lemma

3.10
with a union bound,
\mathsf{E}_{4}
is overwhelmingly probable.

Set
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}\cap\mathsf{E}_{4}
, which by a union bound is overwhelmingly probable. In particular,
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}^{\complement}]<\mathfrak{p}
for sufficiently small
\mathfrak{c}>0
, so we will restrict to
\mathsf{E}
in what follows. By (
5.4
), it then suffices to show that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\big|\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})-\mathfrak{F}(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})\big|\leq AB(\log N)^{5}.

To that end, observe from the definition (
5.2
) of
\mathfrak{F}
and the fact that
\psi
is a bijection that

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\mathfrak{F}(\bm{a};\bm{b})-\mathfrak{F}(\tilde{\bm{a}};\tilde{\bm{b}})|&=\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big(F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{i})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big)\Bigg|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|.\end{aligned}

The following lemma restricts the sum on the right side of (
5.8
) to
i
satisfying
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

On
\mathsf{E}
, we have for sufficiently large
N
that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}&\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|+16AB(\log N)^{3}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

First assume that
\mathcal{D}=\{k\}
for some integer
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Observe for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\leq 2\displaystyle\sup_{|\lambda|\leq\log N}|F(\lambda)|\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{q\in\mathbb{R}}|G(q)|\leq 2\cdot A\cdot 2B\leq 4AB,

where the former bound holds by our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
and the latter by the second and fourth statements in
Assumption

4.1
. This, together with the fact that there are at most
4(\log N)^{3}
indices
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})<2(\log N)^{3}
, yields (
5.9
).

Thus, assume instead that
\mathcal{D}=\{i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:|i-j|>S(\log N)^{9/2}\}
. To verify (
5.9
), it suffices to show that
G(q_{i}-q_{j})=0=G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})
if
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})<2(\log N)^{3}
. To do so, by the fourth statement in
Assumption

4.1
, it suffices to verify that

(5.10)

\displaystyle|q_{i}-q_{j}|\geq 2S,\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\geq 2S,\qquad\text{if $\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\leq 2(\log N)^{3}$}.

If
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\leq 2(\log N)^{3}
, then since
|i^{\prime}-j|\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}
for any
i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
we have

(5.11)

\displaystyle|i-j|\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot S(\log N)^{9/2}.

Hence, by our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, we have
|q_{i}-q_{j}|\geq|\alpha|\cdot|i-j|/2\geq 2S
; this confirms the first statement in (
5.10
). To verify the second, first observe that entirely analogous reasoning to that above confirms it if
\dist(\psi(i),\mathcal{D})\leq 2(\log N)^{3}
. If instead
\dist(\psi(i),\mathcal{D})>2(\log N)^{3}
, then (
5.11
) and (
5.6
) give
|\psi(i)-j|\geq|i-j|-(\log N)^{2}\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}/4
. So, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, we have
|\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\geq|\alpha|\cdot|\psi(i)-j|/2\geq 2S
. This shows (
5.10
) and thus the lemma.
∎

Now we can establish
Lemma

5.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.3
.

First observe that

(5.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}\big|F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})\cdot G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}\big(A\cdot|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})|+2B\cdot|F(\Lambda_{i})-F(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\psi(i)})|\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq A\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})|+2ABNe^{-(\log N)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

Here, in the first bound we used the fact that for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
we have
|F(\Lambda_{i})|\leq A
(by the second statement in (
4.1
) with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
) and that
\big|G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})\big|\leq 2B
(by the fourth statement in (
4.1
)), and in the second we used the third statement of
Assumption

4.1
with (
5.6
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
.

To bound the right side of (
5.12
), we claim when
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}
that

(5.13)

\displaystyle|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}|\leq(\log N)^{5/2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad|q_{i}-q_{j}-\tilde{q}_{i}+\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 12(\log N)^{2}.

Since
|i-\psi(i)|\leq(\log N)^{2}
by (
5.6
), the first bound in (
5.13
) follows from our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
(by using it to bound
|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{k}|
and
|\tilde{q}_{k}-\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}|
for
k=\max\{i+(\log N)^{2},\psi(i)+(\log N)^{2}\}
). To verify the second bound in (
5.13
), observe that

(5.14)

\displaystyle|q_{i}-q_{j}+\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|=2\displaystyle\sum_{m=i}^{j-1}|\log a_{m}-\log\tilde{a}_{m}|\leq 4(\log N)^{2}\cdot\#\{m\in\llbracket i,j-1\rrbracket:\dist(m,\mathcal{D})\leq 1\},

where in the first statement we used (
1.9
), and in the second we used the fact that
a_{m}=\tilde{a}_{m}
unless
\dist(m,\mathcal{D})\leq 1
and our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
(on which each
|\log a_{m}-\log\tilde{a}_{m}|\leq 2(\log N)^{2}
). If
\mathcal{D}=\{k\}
for some
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, then the number of
m\in\llbracket i,j-1\rrbracket
with
\dist(m,\mathcal{D})\leq 1
is at most
3
. If instead
\mathcal{D}=\{h\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket:|h-j|\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}\}
, then this number is equal to
0
, as
\mathcal{D}
then does not intersect
[i-1,j]
(since
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}
). This with (
5.14
) confirms the second bound in (
5.13
).

Using (
5.13
), we estimate the right side of (
5.12
) through the fourth statement in
Assumption

4.1
. Due to presence of the term
B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{x\geq 0}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{y\leq 0}
there, it will be useful to define the set
\mathcal{I}_{1}
of indices
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}
, such that either
q_{i}-q_{j}\geq 0\geq\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}
or
q_{i}-q_{j}\leq 0\leq\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}
. Similarly, we define the set
\mathcal{I}_{2}
of indices
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}
, such that either
\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}\geq 0\geq\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}
or
\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}\leq 0\leq\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}
. Then, due to (
5.13
) and our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, it is quickly verified that
|\mathcal{I}_{1}|\leq(\log N)^{3}
and
|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\leq(\log N)^{3}
. Hence, denoting
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{1}\cup\mathcal{I}_{2}
, we have

(5.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})|\\
&\qquad\leq\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}\big(|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})|+|G(\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})|\big)\\
&\qquad\leq BS^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}\big(|q_{i}-q_{j}-\tilde{q}_{i}+\tilde{q}_{j}|\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{|q_{i}-q_{j}|\leq 2S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}|\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S})\big)+2B\cdot|\mathcal{I}|\\
&\qquad\leq 4BS^{-1}\cdot(\log N)^{5/2}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}(\mathbbm{1}_{|q_{i}-q_{j}|\leq 2S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S})+4B(\log N)^{3},\end{aligned}

where in the first bound we decomposed the sum; in the second we used the fourth statement in (
4.1
) (with the definitions of
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
); and in the third we used (
5.13
) with the fact that that
|\mathcal{I}|\leq|\mathcal{I}_{1}|+|\mathcal{I}_{2}|\leq 2(\log N)^{3}
(and that
N
is sufficiently large). Now, due to our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, there exists a constant
C>1
such that there are at most
CS(\log N)^{2}
indices
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
such that either
|q_{i}-q_{j}|\leq 2S
,
|\tilde{q}_{i}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S
, or
|\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j}|\leq 2S
. Inserting this into (
5.15
) yields

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i:\dist(i,\mathcal{D})\geq 2(\log N)^{3}}|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\tilde{q}_{\psi(i)}-\tilde{q}_{j})|\leq 8CB(\log N)^{9/2},

which with (
5.12
),
Lemma

5.5
, and (
5.8
) shows (
5.7
) and thus the lemma. ∎

5.4.
Proof of
Lemma

4.7

In this section we establish
Lemma

4.7
. To that end, it will be useful to define a bounded variant of
F
; we therefore define the function
H:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
setting

\displaystyle H(\lambda)=F(\lambda)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|F(\lambda)|\leq A}-A\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{F(\lambda)<-A}+A\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{F(\lambda)>A}.

Observe that
|H(\lambda)|\leq A
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, and (by the third property in
Assumption

4.1
) that

(5.16)

\displaystyle|H(x)-H(y)|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}},\quad\text{for any $x,y\in[-\log N,\log N]$ with $|x-y|\leq e^{-(\log N)^{5/2}}$}.

We first compare the expectation of the sum of
F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})
to that of
H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})
.

Lemma 5.6
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that

(5.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(&q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]\Bigg|\leq c^{-1}ABe^{-c(\log N)^{2}};\\
&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F(\lambda)-H(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\leq c^{-1}Ae^{-c(\log N)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

Recalling
Definition

3.12
, let
\mathsf{E}_{0}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
. To show the first statement of the (
5.17
), observe for some
c_{1}>0
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\big(F(\Lambda_{i})-H(\Lambda_{i})\big)\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle=2B\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}|F(\lambda_{i})|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}_{0}^{\complement}}\Bigg]\leq 2BN\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}}|F(\lambda)|^{2}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{E}_{0}^{\complement}]^{1/2}\leq c_{1}^{-1}ABe^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{2}},

where the first statement follows from the facts that
|G(q)|\leq 2B
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
and (
5.16
) (which in particular implies that
F(\lambda)=H(\lambda)
whenever
|\lambda|\leq\log N
, by
Assumption

4.1
), with the definition of
\mathsf{BND}
; the second from bounding each term in the sum over
\lambda
by its maximum; and the third from
Lemma

3.13
and (the
v=2
case of)
Lemma

5.4
. This shows the first bound in (
5.17
).

To confirm the second, observe for some
c_{2},c_{3}>0
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F(\lambda)-H(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{\lambda:|F(\lambda)|>A}|F(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{|\lambda|>\log N}|F(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda

\displaystyle\leq A\displaystyle\int_{|\lambda|>\log N}e^{|\lambda|^{1/2}-c_{2}|\lambda|^{2}}d\lambda\leq Ae^{-c_{3}(\log N)^{2}},

where the first inequality follows from (
5.16
); the second from the second statement in
Assumption

4.1
; the third from the first statement in
Assumption

4.1
, with
Lemma

3.1
; and the fourth from performing the integration.
∎

The following lemma approximates the expectation of the sum of
H(\Lambda_{i})
over some interval
i\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
; if
\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket=\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, it may be thought of as a variant of
Lemma

3.14
with an effective error. We establish it in
Appendix

D
below.

Lemma 5.7
.

Fix integers
n_{1},n_{2}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
n_{2}\geq n_{1}
; denote
n=n_{2}-n_{1}+1
, and assume that
n\geq(\log N)^{5}
. For
N
sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\bigg]-n\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\leq A(\log N)^{6}.

The following lemma approximates “local averages” of
H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})
, that is, over intervals
i\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
of essentially arbitrary size.

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix integers
n_{1},n_{2}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
n_{2}\geq n_{1}
; set
n=n_{2}-n_{1}+1
, and assume that
n\geq(\log N)^{5}
. For
N
sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(

\displaystyle q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\quad\leq AB\big(S^{-1/2}n(\log N)^{9/2}+S^{-1}n^{2}\log N+2\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|n_{1}-j|\leq 2n}\big).

Proof.

First observe that

(5.18)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\Bigg|\leq A\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)|,

where we used the fact that
|H(\lambda)|\leq A
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. To estimate the right side of (
5.18
), we define the event

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}=\bigcap_{i,i^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}\{|q_{i}-q_{i^{\prime}}-\alpha(i-i^{\prime})|\leq|i-i^{\prime}|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\}.

By
Lemma

3.10
and a union bound,
\mathsf{F}
holds with overwhelming probability.

Let us observe two inequalities on the event
\mathsf{F}
. The first is

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{F}}\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{|q_{i}-q_{j}|\leq S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\alpha i-\alpha j|\leq S})\cdot|i-j|\leq S(\log N)^{5},

which holds since if
|i-j|\geq S(\log N)^{5}
and
\mathsf{F}
holds then
|q_{i}-q_{j}|\geq|\alpha i-\alpha j|-|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\geq|\alpha|S(\log N)^{5}-S^{1/2}(\log N)^{9/2}>S
and
|\alpha i-\alpha j|\geq|\alpha|S(\log N)^{5}>S
. The second is that, on
\mathsf{F}
, the bounds
q_{i}\leq q_{j}
and
\alpha i\geq\alpha j
can both hold only if
|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}
. Indeed, suppose to the contrary that on
\mathsf{F}
the former two inequalities held, in addition to
|i-j|>(\log N)^{5}
. Then, we would have
0\geq q_{i}-q_{j}\geq\alpha(i-j)-|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}>0
, as
|i-j|>(\log N)^{5}
, which is a contradiction. Similarly, on
\mathsf{F}
, the bounds
q_{i}\geq q_{j}
and
\alpha i\leq\alpha j
can both hold only if
|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}
.

Therefore, for any
i\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\alpha i-\alpha j)|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle\leq BS^{-1}\cdot|i-j|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{|q_{i}-q_{j}|\leq S}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\alpha i-\alpha j|\leq S})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle\qquad+B\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{q_{i}\geq q_{j}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\alpha i\leq\alpha j}+\mathbbm{1}_{q_{i}\leq q_{j}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\alpha i\geq\alpha j})

\displaystyle\leq BS^{-1/2}(\log N)^{9/2}+B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}},

where the first statement holds by the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
(with the definition of
\mathsf{F}
), and the second holds by the above two inequalities that hold on
\mathsf{F}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G

\displaystyle(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{F}}

\displaystyle\leq BS^{-1/2}(\log N)^{9/2}+B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}}+|G(\alpha i-\alpha j)-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)|

\displaystyle\leq BS^{-1/2}(\log N)^{9/2}+B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}}+|\alpha|BS^{-1}n+B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{i\geq j\geq n_{1}},

where we again used the fourth statement of
Assumption

4.1
, with the fact that
|i-n_{1}|\leq n
. Summing over
i\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
, this gives (since and
i\geq j\geq n_{1}
implies
|n_{1}-j|\leq n
, and
|i-j|\leq(\log N)^{5}
implies
|n_{1}-j|\leq n+(\log N)^{5}\leq 2n
, as
n\geq(\log N)^{5}
) that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{F}}\leq BS^{-1/2}n(\log N)^{9/2}+|\alpha|BS^{-1}n^{2}+2B\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|n_{1}-j|\leq 2n}.

The lemma then follows from inserting this into (
5.18
); taking expectations; using the fact that
|G(q_{i}-q_{j})-G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j)|\leq 2B
deterministically (by the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
); and using the fact that
\mathsf{F}
holds with overwhelming probability.
∎

We can now establish
Lemma

4.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

By
Lemma

5.8
and
Lemma

5.7
(with the fact that
|G(q)|\leq 2B
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
), we find for any
n_{1},n_{2}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
with
n=n_{2}-n_{1}+1\leq\lceil S^{1/2}(\log N)^{5}\rceil
that

(5.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]-n\cdot G(\alpha n_{1}-\alpha j&)\cdot\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\\
&\leq 2AB\big((\log N)^{11}+\mathbbm{1}_{|n_{1}-j|\leq 2n}\big).\end{aligned}

We will first apply (
5.20
) for a family of intervals
\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
covering a neighborhood of
j
(of size about
2S(\log N)^{9/2}
). Thus, let
r\leq S^{1/2}
and
n_{1,1}<n_{1,2}<\cdots<n_{1,r}
be integers with
n_{1,i+1}-n_{1,i}=n=\lceil S^{1/2}(\log N)^{5}\rceil
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,r-1\rrbracket
and
n_{1,1}\leq j-S(\log N)^{9/2}<j+S(\log N)^{9/2}\leq n_{1,r}
. Applying (
5.20
), with the
(n_{1},n_{2})
there equal to
(n_{1,m},n_{1,m+1})
here, and summing over
m\in\llbracket 1,r-1\rrbracket
, we obtain

(5.21)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1,1}}^{n_{1,r}}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]-n\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)\cdot\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\leq 3ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{11},

where we used the facts that
r\leq S^{1/2}
and that there are at most
5
values of
m\in\llbracket 1,r\rrbracket
for which
|n_{1,m}-j|\leq 2n
.

Next, we must estimate the expectation of
H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})
when
i\notin\llbracket n_{1,1},n_{1,r}\rrbracket
; observe for such
i
that we have
|i-j|\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}
. To that end, recall the event
\mathsf{F}
from (
5.19
), which holds with overwhelming probability, by
Lemma

3.10
(and a union bound). On
\mathsf{F}
, we have that
|q_{i}-q_{j}|\geq 2S
whenever
|i-j|\geq S(\log N)^{9/2}
; in particular, we have
G(q_{i}-q_{j})=0
on
\mathsf{F}
if
i\notin\llbracket n_{1,1},n_{1,r}\rrbracket
. Together with the deterministic bounds
|H(\Lambda)|\leq A
for all
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
|G(q)|\leq 2B
(applied off of
\mathsf{F}
), we find that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i\notin\llbracket n_{1,1},n_{2,r}\rrbracket}H(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]\Bigg|\leq 2c_{1}^{-1}ABNe^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{2}}\leq ABN^{-1}.

Together with (
5.21
) and
Lemma

5.6
, this gives

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}F(\Lambda_{i})\cdot G(q_{i}-q_{j})\bigg]-n\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)\cdot\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}F(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\leq 4ABS^{1/2}(\log N)^{11}.

Since

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|F(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|H(\lambda)|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda+A\leq 2A,

where the first inequality holds from
Lemma

5.6
, and the second holds from the facts that
|H(\lambda)|\leq A
and that
\varrho
is a probability measure, we must show that

\displaystyle\Bigg|n\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(\alpha q)dq\Bigg|\leq BS^{1/2}(\log N)^{11}.

Since
r\leq S^{1/2}
; since
n\leq 2S^{1/2}(\log N)^{5}
; since
G(\alpha q-\alpha j)=0
for
q\notin[n_{1,1},n_{1,r}]
(by
Assumption

4.1
); and since there are at most
2
indices
m\in\llbracket 1,r-1\rrbracket
for which
n_{1,m+1}\geq j\geq n_{1,m}
, it suffices to show for any
m\in\llbracket 1,r-1\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|n\cdot G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)-\displaystyle\int_{n_{1,m}}^{n_{1,m+1}}G(\alpha q-\alpha j)dq\Bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{B}{10}\cdot(\log N)^{11}+Bn\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{n_{1,m+1}\geq j\geq n_{1,m}}.

This follows from the bounds

\displaystyle\Bigg|n\cdot G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j

\displaystyle)-\displaystyle\int_{n_{1,m}}^{n_{1,m+1}}G(\alpha q-\alpha j)dq\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|(n_{1,m+1}-n_{1,m})\cdot G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)-\displaystyle\int_{n_{1,m}}^{n_{1,m+1}}G(\alpha q-\alpha j)dq\Bigg|+2B

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot\displaystyle\max_{q\in[n_{1,m},n_{1,m+1}]}|G(\alpha n_{1,m}-\alpha j)-G(\alpha q-\alpha j)|+2B

\displaystyle\leq BS^{-1}n\cdot|\alpha n_{1,m+1}-\alpha n_{1,m}|+Bn\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{n_{1,m+1}\geq j\geq n_{1,m}}+2B

\displaystyle\leq 4|\alpha|B(\log N)^{10}+Bn\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{n_{1,m+1}\geq j\geq n_{1,m}}+2B,

where in the first inequality we used the facts that
|G(q)|\leq 2B
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
and that
n=n_{1,m+1}-n_{1,m}+1
; in the second we bounded the integral by its maximum (and used the fact that
n\geq n_{1,m+1}-n_{1,m}
); in the third we used the fourth part of
Assumption

4.1
; and in the fourth we used the fact that
n_{1,m+1}-n_{1,m}\leq n\leq 2S^{1/2}(\log N)^{5}
.
∎

6.
Regularization and Matrix Bounds

In
Section

6.1
we use the concentration bound
Proposition

4.4
to derive a “regularized” variant of the asymptotic scattering relation
Proposition

3.23
(along the lines of (
2.7
)), given by
Proposition

6.2
. In
Section

6.2
we discuss properties of a matrix
\bm{S}
(defined in (
6.6
)) that will eventually arise from formally differentiating this regularized relation.

6.1.
Regularized Asymptotic Scattering Relation

In this section we show a variant of the asymptotic scattering relation
Proposition

3.23
, in which the restrictions on
i
and logarithms in the sums in (
3.9
) are incorporated through more regular functions
\chi
and
\mathfrak{l}
, respectively. To state this more precisely, we require some notation.

Assumption 6.1
.

Adopt
Assumption

1.12
, and fix real numbers
B\geq 10
and
\mathfrak{M}\in[1,T]
. Let
\chi=\chi_{\mathfrak{M}}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a smooth function with
\chi^{\prime}
even and nonnegative, satisfying the following two properties.

(1)

If
|x|\geq\mathfrak{M}
then
\chi(x)=\sgn(\alpha)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{x>0}
.

(2)

For each
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, we have
|\partial_{x}^{k}\chi(x)|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{-k}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Further set
\mathfrak{d}=e^{-5(\log N)^{2}}
, and define the function
\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{l}_{\mathfrak{d}}:\mathbb{R}
by for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
setting

(6.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}(x)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log|x^{2}+\mathfrak{d}^{2}|.

We then have the following regularized version of
Proposition

3.23
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Adopt
Assumption

6.1
, and fix
t\in[0,T]
. The following holds with overwhelming probability. For any index
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
3.8
), we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\lambda_{k}t-Q_{k}(t)+Q_{k}(0)-2\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i})\cdot\big(\chi(Q_{k}(t)-Q_{i}(t))-\chi(&Q_{k}(0)-Q_{i}(0))\big)\Bigg|\\
&\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{16}.\end{aligned}

We establish
Proposition

6.2
as a quick consequence of
Proposition

3.23
and the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Fix
t\in[0,T]
. The following holds with overwhelming probability. For any
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
satisfying (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\sgn(\alpha)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{t}(i)<Q_{t}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|-\sgn(\alpha)\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{0}(i)<Q_{0}(k)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i})\cdot\big(\chi(Q_{k}(t)-Q_{i}(t))-\chi(Q_{k}(0)-Q_{i}(0))\big)\Bigg|\leq 11B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{15}.

Proof of
Proposition

6.2
.

This follows from
Proposition

3.23
and
Lemma

6.3
.
∎

Now we prove
Lemma

6.3
using
Proposition

4.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.3
.

Throughout, we assume for notational convenience that
\alpha>0
, as the proof when
\alpha<0
is entirely analogous. We apply
Proposition

4.4
with the
F,G:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
there defined by setting
F(\lambda)=1
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, setting
f(x)=\mathfrak{l}(x)
, and setting
G(q)=\chi(-q)-\mathbbm{1}_{q<0}
(if
\alpha<0
, then we instead set
G(q)=\chi(-q)+\mathbbm{1}_{q<0}
). Observe in this way that
F
,
f
, and
G
satisfy
Assumption

4.1
, (
4.3
), and (
4.4
) with the
(A,B,D,S)
there equal to
(1,B,5(\log N)^{2},\mathfrak{M})
here, by
Assumption

6.1
. Moreover, since
\chi^{\prime}
is even and
G(q)=\chi(-q)-\mathbbm{1}_{q<0}
is compactly supported,
G(q)
is odd in
q
(away from
q=0
), which means that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(q)dq=0
. Therefore
Proposition

4.4
yields an overwhelmingly probable event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
, on which we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{i}(s)<Q_{k}(s)}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k})-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k})\cdot\chi(Q_{k}(s)-Q_{i}(s))\Bigg|\leq 5B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{15}.

Subtracting this estimate at
s=t
from that at
s=0
yields on
\mathsf{E}_{1}
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{i}(t)<Q_{k}(t)}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k})-\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{i}(0)<Q_{k}(0)}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k})

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{k})\cdot\big(\chi(Q_{k}(t)-Q_{i}(t))-\chi(Q_{k}(0)-Q_{i}(0))\big)\Bigg|\leq 10B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{15}.

It therefore remains to show for each
s\in\{0,t\}
that, with overwhelming probability,

(6.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{i}(s)<Q_{k}(s)}\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i})-\displaystyle\sum_{i:Q_{i}(s)<Q_{k}(s)}\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\Bigg|\leq 1.\end{aligned}

To that end, recalling
Definition

3.12
, define the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}=\mathsf{SEP}_{\bm{L}(0)}(e^{-(\log N)^{2}})
. By
Lemma

3.13
,
\mathsf{E}_{2}
holds with overwhelming probability. We thus restrict to
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}
in what follows. To verify (
6.3
), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\big|\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i})-\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\big|\leq 1.

This follows from the fact that
|\mathfrak{l}(\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i})-\log|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}||\leq\mathfrak{d}^{2}|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|^{-2}\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
(as
|\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{i}|\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
).
∎

6.2.
Matrix Bounds

In this section we define and discuss properties of a certain family of matrices that will be useful in analyzing the asymptotic scattering relation
Proposition

6.2
. These matrices are provided by the following definition. Observe that (
6.4
) below imposes a more stringent constraint on
T
than in
Assumption

1.12
; we will remove it when proving
Theorem

1.13
in
Section

8.3
. While we will not impose this in what follows, it will be useful to think of
B=\mathcal{O}(1)
and
\mathfrak{M}\sim T
.

Assumption 6.4
.

Adopt
Assumption

6.1
. Assume that

(6.4)

\displaystyle B\in[10,N^{1/500}],\quad T\in[B^{4}(\log N)^{60},N^{1/10}],\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{M}\in[B^{4}(\log N)^{60},T].

Further fix indices
k_{1},k_{2}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
satisfying

(6.5)

\displaystyle N_{1}+T(\log N)^{7}\leq k_{1}\leq-N(\log N)^{-10};\quad N(\log N)^{-10}\leq k_{2}\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{7}.

For any integers
\ell,m\in\llbracket k_{1},k_{2}\rrbracket
with

\displaystyle k_{1}\leq\ell\leq-N(\log N)^{-9};\qquad N(\log N)^{-9}\leq m\leq k_{2},

and
N
-tuples
\bm{\Lambda}=(\Lambda_{N_{1}},\Lambda_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\Lambda_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}=(\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{1}},\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, define the matrix
\bm{S}=\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}}^{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}=[S_{ij}]=[S_{ij;\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}
by for any
i,j\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
setting

(6.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} S_{ij}&=\Bigg(2\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})+1\Bigg)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{i=j}-2\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{i})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i})\\
&\qquad+T^{3}\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{i\leq\ell+T^{2}}+\mathbbm{1}_{i\geq m+T^{2}})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{i=j}.\end{aligned}

Let us briefly explain the origin of the matrix
\bm{S}
given by
Assumption

6.4
. Consider the asymptotic scattering relation (
6.2
); ignore the error; and formally differentiate it in
t
. This yields a system of linear equations for
Q_{j}^{\prime}(t)
, whose coefficients are essentially the entries of
\bm{S}
(except for the
(i,j)
coordinates with
i=j\in\llbracket\ell,\ell+T^{2}\rrbracket\cup\llbracket m-T^{2},m\rrbracket
, in which case
S_{ij}
is
T^{3}
larger; these additional boundary terms are for convenience in the proofs and will not affect the asymptotics). To solve for the
Q_{j}^{\prime}(t)
, one must therefore invert the matrix
\bm{S}
, and estimate its inverse. While we do not know how to do this in general (see
Remark

6.7
below), it can be done under the following assumption (
6.7
) on its entries.

Lemma 6.5
.

Adopt
Assumption

6.4
, and fix any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Suppose for each
j\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
that

(6.7)

\displaystyle|S_{jj}|\geq(2+\varepsilon)\displaystyle\sum_{i=\ell}^{m}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{i})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i})|+\varepsilon.

(1)

The matrix
\bm{S}_{\bm{\mathfrak{Q}};\bm{\Lambda}}^{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}
is invertible, with inverse
\bm{R}=\bm{R}_{\bm{\mathfrak{Q}};\bm{\Lambda}}^{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}=[R_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}
.

(2)

Let
\bm{v}=(v_{\ell},v_{\ell+1},\ldots,v_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m-\ell+1}
, and denote
\bm{R}\bm{v}=\bm{w}=(w_{\ell},w_{\ell+1},\ldots,w_{m})
. Then, for any
i\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
and
U\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
, we have

(6.8)

\displaystyle|w_{i}|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k:|\mathfrak{Q}_{k}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i}|\leq U\mathfrak{M}}|v_{i}|+\varepsilon^{-1}e^{-\varepsilon U/8}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}|v_{k}|.

Proof.

By (
6.6
), the bound (
6.7
) guarantees that the matrix
\bm{S}^{\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}
is strictly diagonally dominant and is thus invertible. It remains to establish the second statement of the lemma.

To that end, set
\delta=\varepsilon/2
, and let
i_{0}\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
denote an index such that
|w_{k}|\leq(1+\delta)\cdot|w_{i_{0}}|
, whenever
k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
satisfies
|\mathfrak{Q}_{k}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}|\leq\mathfrak{M}
. Then, we have

(6.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |v_{i_{0}}|&\geq|w_{i_{0}}|\cdot|S_{i_{0}i_{0}}|-2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{k}-\Lambda_{i})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})\cdot w_{k}|\\
&\geq\varepsilon|w_{i_{0}}|+2(1+\delta)\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{i_{0}}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})|\cdot(|w_{i_{0}}|-(1+\delta)^{-1}\cdot|w_{k}|)\geq\varepsilon|w_{i_{0}}|,\end{aligned}

where in the first statement we used (
6.6
); in the second we used (
6.7
), with the fact that
2\delta=\varepsilon
; and in the third we used the fact that
|w_{k}|\leq(1+\delta)\cdot|w_{i_{0}}|
whenever
|\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}|\leq\mathfrak{M}
(and that
\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})=0
whenever
|\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{0}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}|>\mathfrak{M}
, by
Assumption

6.1
).

Now assume to the contrary that (
6.8
) does not hold, and set
i_{1}=i
. Then, by the
i_{0}=i_{1}
case of (
6.9
), we either have that
|w_{i_{1}}|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot|v_{i_{1}}|
or that there exists some index
i_{2}\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
with
|\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{2}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{1}}|\leq\mathfrak{M}
such that
|w_{i_{2}}|\geq(1+\delta)\cdot|w_{i_{1}}|
. The former would imply (
6.8
) and therefore cannot hold, so the latter must. Applying (
6.9
) again, now at
i_{0}=i_{2}
, it follows that either
|w_{i_{1}}|\leq|w_{i_{2}}|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot|v_{i_{2}}|
or that there exists some index
i_{3}\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
with
|\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{3}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{1}}|\leq\mathfrak{M}+|\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{3}}-\mathfrak{Q}_{i_{2}}|\leq 2\mathfrak{M}
such that
|w_{i_{3}}|\geq(1+\delta)\cdot|w_{i_{2}}|\geq(1+\delta)^{2}\cdot|w_{i_{1}}|
. The former again would imply (
6.8
) and therefore cannot hold, so the second does. Repeating in this way, and setting
K=\lfloor U\rfloor+1
, it follows that there exist an index
i_{K}\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
satisfying
|w_{i_{K}}|\geq(1+\delta)^{\lfloor U\rfloor}\cdot|w_{i_{1}}|
. Hence,

\displaystyle|w_{i}|\leq(1+\delta)^{-\lfloor U\rfloor}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}|w_{k}|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{-1}(1+\delta)^{-U/2}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}|v_{k}|\leq\varepsilon^{-1}e^{-\varepsilon U/8}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}|v_{k}|,

where the first bound follows from the fact that
|w_{i_{K}}|\geq(1+\delta)^{\lfloor U\rfloor}\cdot|w_{i_{1}}|
; the second from (
6.9
) (with the
i_{0}
there equal to
k
for which
|w_{k}|
is maximal, which implies (
6.9
)); and the third from the fact that
1+\delta\geq e^{\delta/2}=e^{\varepsilon/4}
for
\delta\in(0,1)
. Thus, (
6.8
) holds, which is a contradiction; this confirms the lemma.
∎

To use
Lemma

6.5
, we will need to verify the bound (
6.7
) when the
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}
are close to the
Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}
. For general values of
\theta
(such as if
\beta
is very large in comparison to
\theta^{-1}
), it can be verified that this bound might in fact be false. However,
Lemma

3.9
will be used to show that it does hold if
\theta
is sufficiently small.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the below holds. Adopt
Assumption

6.4
, and fix a real number
s\in[0,T]
. The following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Let
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\beta)>0
be the real number from
Lemma

3.9
, and suppose that
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
. Assume for each
j\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
that
\Lambda_{j}=\lambda_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}
, and that

(6.10)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)|\leq(\log N)^{-10}B^{-1}\mathfrak{M}.

Then, (
6.7
) holds with the
\varepsilon
there equal to
(\log N)^{-1}
here.

Proof.

We begin by introducing several events on whose intersection we will be able to verify (
6.7
). Recalling
Definition

3.12
, define the events
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
and

(6.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{E}_{2}&=\bigcap_{j=\ell}^{m}\Bigg\{\bigg|2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s))\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)\varrho(x)dx\bigg|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{-1/2}(\log N)^{16}\Bigg\};\\
\mathsf{E}_{3}&=\bigcap_{j=\ell}^{m}\Bigg\{\bigg|2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s))|\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)\big|\varrho(x)dx\bigg|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{-1/2}(\log N)^{16}\Bigg\};\\
\mathsf{E}_{4}&=\bigcap_{j=1}^{N}\{|\varphi_{j}(s)-\varphi_{j}(0)|\leq T(\log N)^{2}\};\qquad\mathsf{E}_{5}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\\
|i-j|\geq T(\log N)^{5}\end{subarray}}\bigg\{|q_{i}(s)-q_{j}(s)|\geq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot|i-j|\bigg\};\\
\mathsf{E}_{6}&=\bigcap_{i,j\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket}\big\{\big|q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i)}(s)-\alpha(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)-\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i))\big|\leq|\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i)-\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)|^{1/2}\cdot(\log N)^{2}\big\}.\end{aligned}

Observe by
Lemma

3.13
(and
Lemma

3.21
, with (
6.5
), to verify (
4.1
)) that
\mathsf{E}_{1}
is overwhelmingly probable. By the
(F,G;A,B,D,S)=(1,\chi^{\prime};1,(\log N)^{3},B\mathfrak{M}^{-1},\mathfrak{M})
and
f\in\{\mathfrak{l},|\mathfrak{l}|\}
cases of
Proposition

4.4
(with
Assumption

6.1
to verify its hypotheses), and the fact that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\chi^{\prime}(\alpha q)dq=\alpha^{-1}\cdot(\chi(\mathfrak{M})-\chi(-\mathfrak{M}))=\alpha^{-1},

\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}
is overwhelmingly probable. By
Lemma

3.21
,
\mathsf{E}_{4}
is overwhelmingly probable and, by
Lemma

3.11
,
\mathsf{E}_{5}
is also overwhelmingly probable. By
Lemma

3.11
, with the fact that on
\mathsf{E}_{4}
we have
\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k))\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
for
k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
(by (
6.5
), as
|\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k))-k|\leq 2T(\log N)^{2}
for such
k
on
\mathsf{E}_{4}
),
\mathsf{E}_{6}
is overwhelmingly probable. Therefore, by a union bound, we restrict to
\mathsf{E}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{6}\mathsf{E}_{i}
below.

First observe if
\ell+T^{2}\leq j\leq m-T^{2}
and
k\notin\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
that

(6.12)

\displaystyle|Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)|\geq\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{4}\cdot T^{2}>\mathfrak{M},

where in the first inequality we used the fact (
1.15
) that
Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i)}(s)=q_{\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i))}(s)
, our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{5}
, and the bound

\displaystyle|\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))-\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k))|\geq|j-k|-4T(\log N)^{2}\geq T^{2}-4T(\log N)^{2}\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot T^{2},

which holds by our restriction to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
, the fact that
|j-k|\geq T^{2}
for
j\in\llbracket\ell+T^{2},m-T^{2}\rrbracket
and
k\notin\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
, and (
6.4
); in the second inequality, we used the fact (recall
Assumption

6.1
) that
\mathfrak{M}\leq T
. It follows for
\ell+T^{2}\leq j\leq m-T^{2}
that

(6.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |S_{jj}|&=\Bigg|2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})+1\Bigg|-10B\mathfrak{M}^{-1}(\log N)^{2}\\
&\geq\Bigg|2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}\big(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)\big)+1\Bigg|\\
&\qquad\qquad-10B\mathfrak{M}^{-2}(\log N)^{2}\cdot 2B^{-1}\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{-10}\cdot\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{5}-10B\mathfrak{M}^{-1}(\log N)^{2}\\
&\geq\Bigg|2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}\big(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)\big)+1\Bigg|\\
&\qquad\qquad-10B\mathfrak{M}^{-2}(\log N)^{2}\cdot 2B^{-1}\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{-10}\cdot\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{5}-10B\mathfrak{M}^{-1}(\log N)^{2}\\
&\geq\Bigg|2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)\varrho(x)dx+1\Bigg|-(\log N)^{-2}\geq 2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)|\varrho(x)+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4},\end{aligned}

where in the first statement we used (
6.6
); in the second we used (
6.10
), the fact that
\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\leq 5(\log N)^{2}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
), the fact that
|\chi^{\prime\prime}(q)|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{-2}
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}
(by the second statement of
Assumption

6.1
), and the fact that there are at most
\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{5}
indices
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
for which
|Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)|\leq\mathfrak{M}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}\cap\mathsf{E}_{6}
) and thus
\chi^{\prime}(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s))\neq 0
; in the third we used with (
6.12
) and the fact that
\supp\chi^{\prime}\subseteq[-\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{M}]
to sum over all
k\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
(and not only over
k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
); and in the fourth we used our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
, with (
6.4
); and in the fourth we used
Lemma

3.9
, with the fact that
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
.

Reversing the reasoning in (
6.13
) (and using our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
in place of that to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
), we find for
\varepsilon=(\log N)^{-1}
that

(6.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)|\varrho(x)dx+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}&\geq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s))+\displaystyle\frac{1}{5}\\
&\geq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{j})+\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\\
&\geq(2+\varepsilon)\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{j})+\varepsilon.\end{aligned}

Here, in the last bound we used the fact that there exists a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})\leq 2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-x)|\varrho(x)dx+\displaystyle\frac{1}{12}<C,

where the first inequality holds by the first two bounds of (
6.14
) and the second holds since
\varrho
is bounded and has exponential decay (by
Lemma

3.1
). Thus, if
\ell+T^{2}\leq j\leq m-T^{2}
, then (
6.13
) and (
6.14
) confirm (
6.7
) at
\varepsilon=(\log N)^{-1}
.

Now suppose that
j\notin\llbracket\ell+T^{2},m-T^{2}\rrbracket
. In this case, the third statement in (
6.13
) is no longer guaranteed to hold, as we do not necessarily have
\chi^{\prime}(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s))=0
for all
k\notin\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
. So, we instead make use of the last term of order
T^{3}
on the right side of the definition (
6.6
) of
S_{ij}
. To do so, first observe that there are at most
3T(\log N)^{2}
indices
k\in\llbracket\ell,m\rrbracket
for which
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}|\leq\mathfrak{M}
(and thus for which
\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})\neq 0
), as for
|\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)-\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i)|\geq T(\log N)^{5}
we have by (
6.10
) and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}|

\displaystyle\geq|Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)|-\mathfrak{M}\geq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot|\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)-\varphi_{0}^{-1}(i)|-\mathfrak{M}\geq\displaystyle\frac{|\alpha|}{2}\cdot T(\log N)^{4}>\mathfrak{M}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle S_{jj}

\displaystyle\geq T^{3}-2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})

\displaystyle\geq T^{3}-5(\log N)^{2}\cdot 3T(\log N)^{5}

\displaystyle\geq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot T^{3}\geq 3\cdot 5(\log N)^{2}\cdot 3T(\log N)^{5}\cdot T+1\geq 3\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell}^{m}|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})+1,

where the first bound holds by (
6.6
); the second and fifth hold by the bounds
|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\leq 5(\log N)^{2}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
) and
|\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k})|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{-1}\leq 1
(in view of (
6.4
)); and the third and fouth by (
6.4
) and the fact that
N
is sufficiently large. This again verifies (
6.7
) at
\varepsilon=1>(\log N)^{-1}
, thereby establishing the lemma.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Our reason for imposing
\theta\leq\theta_{0}
below will be to apply
Lemma

6.6
. By
Lemma

6.5
, this verifies that, when the
(\mathfrak{Q}_{k})
are close to the
(Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)})
, the matrix
\bm{S}
is likely invertible, with eigenvalues essentially bounded away from
0
. One might hope that this statement more generally holds, for some choice of
\chi
satisfying
Assumption

6.1
, without imposing the constraint (
6.7
). If this were true, we would not need to assume that
\theta
is sufficiently small in the below analysis.

7.
Proxy Dynamics and Comparisons

In this section we use the results of
Section

6
to prescribe certain dynamics
(\mathfrak{Q}_{k}(t))
, which (as mentioned in
Section

2.2
) will serve as a “proxy” for the eigenvalue location dynamics
(Q_{k}(t))
. We first define these proxy dynamics by
Definition

7.1
in
Section

7.1
; we then prove that they are close to the
(Q_{j}(t))
dynamics through
Proposition

7.3
, in
Section

7.2
and
Section

7.3
. Throughout this section, we adopt
Assumption

6.4
.

7.1.
Proxy Dynamics

As indicated following
Assumption

6.4
, we would like to proceed by differentiating the asymptotic scattering relation (
6.2
) in
t
. However, the error on the right side of that bound is not necessarily differentiable. So we will instead introduce a “proxy” dynamic
(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(t))
, in which that error is not present, and show that they are close to
(Q_{j}(t))
. We will not be able to verify this approximation for the extreme (first and last few) indices, so it will be convenient to define
(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(t))
on time intervals of the form
t\in[i,i+1]
, reducing the number of indices
j
each time
i
increments. Recall we adopt
Assumption

6.4
thoughout.

Definition 7.1
.

For each integer
i\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, denote
\ell_{i}=k_{1}+iT^{3}
and
m_{i}=k_{2}-iT^{3}
. Further define
\bm{\Lambda}=(\Lambda_{k_{1}},\Lambda_{k_{1}+1},\ldots,\Lambda_{k_{2}})
by
\Lambda_{j}=\lambda_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}
for each
j\in\llbracket k_{1},k_{2}\rrbracket
. Then define the
N
-tuple
\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)=(\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{1}}(s),\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{1}+1}(s),\ldots,\mathfrak{Q}_{N_{2}}(s))\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
as follows. At
s=0
, set
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(0)=Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(0)=q_{j}(0)
for each
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Now suppose
\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)
has been defined for all
s\in[0,i]
, for some index
i\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
.

(1)

If
j\notin\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
, then set
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)=\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(i)
for each
s\geq i
.

(2)

If instead
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
, then for
s\in[i,i+1]
let
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)
be the unique solution to the system of ordinary differential equations

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(s)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell_{i}}^{m_{i}}R_{jk;s}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}\cdot\Lambda_{k},

where
\bm{R}_{s}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}=[R_{jk;s}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}]=(\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket})^{-1}
, with initial data
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(i)
determined by the fact that
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)
has been defined at
s=i
. Here, we assume under the above definition that
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(r)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
is invertible if
r\in[i,i+1]
, so that the Picard–Lindelöf theorem guarantees that (
7.1
) has a unique solution. If
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(r)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
is not invertible for some
r\in[i,i+1]
, we set
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)=\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(i)
whenever
s\in[i,i+1]
.

Remark 7.2
.

Observe that (
7.1
) is equivalent, upon multplying by
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
(using the facts that
\mathfrak{l}
and
\chi^{\prime}
are even) to the equation

(7.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Lambda_{j}&=\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(s)\cdot\Bigg(2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell_{i}}^{m_{i}}\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}(s))+1+T^{3}\cdot(\mathbbm{1}_{j\leq\ell_{i}+T^{2}}+\mathbbm{1}_{j\geq m_{i}-T^{2}})\Bigg)\\
&\qquad-2\displaystyle\sum_{k=\ell_{i}}^{m_{i}}\mathfrak{Q}_{k}^{\prime}(s)\cdot\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})\cdot\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}(s)).\end{aligned}

7.2.
Comparison Between
\bm{Q}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}

In this section we establish the following proposition indicating that
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)
approximates
Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)
with high probability. Recall we adopt
Assumption

6.4
(and use the notation from
Definition

7.1
) throughout.

Proposition 7.3
.

The following holds with overwhelming probability. Let
i\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
be an integer, and let
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\beta)>0
denote the real number from
Lemma

3.9
; assume that
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
.

(1)

For any
r\in[i,i+1]
, the matrix
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(r)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
is invertible.

(2)

For any
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
and
t\in[0,i+1]
, we have
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(t)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(t)|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{20}
and
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
.

We begin by, for each
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
and
s\in[0,T]
, setting

(7.3)

\displaystyle w_{j}(s)=\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(t)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(t).

Further let
\mathfrak{T}
be the supremum over all
S\in[0,T]
such that we have both

(7.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{3},\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for each $t\in(0,S)$ and $j\in\llbracket\ell_{\lfloor S\rfloor},m_{\lfloor S\rfloor}\rrbracket$};\\
&|w_{j}(t)|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{20},\qquad\text{for each $t\in(0,S)$ and $j\in\llbracket\ell_{\lfloor S\rfloor},m_{\lfloor S\rfloor}\rrbracket$}.\end{aligned}

The following two results quickly imply
Proposition

7.3
. In the below, for any
r\in[0,T]
, we write
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(r)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}=[S_{ij}(r)]
if the index
i\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
is given.

Lemma 7.4
.

The following holds with overwhelming probability. For any
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
, we have

(7.5)

\displaystyle S_{jj}(s)\geq(1+(2\log N)^{-1})\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}|S_{jk}(s)|+(2\log N)^{-1},\quad\text{for all $(j,s)\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket\times[0,\mathfrak{T}]$.}

Furthermore, the matrix
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
is invertible for each
s\in[0,\mathfrak{T}]
.

Proposition 7.5
.

With overwhelming probability, we have
\mathfrak{T}=T
.

Proof of
Proposition

7.3
.

This follows from (the last statement of)
Lemma

7.4
,
Proposition

7.5
, and the definition of
\mathfrak{T}
as the supremum over all
S\in[0,T]
satisfying (
7.4
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.4
.

Throughout this proof, recalling
Definition

3.12
, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
, which we may by
Lemma

3.13
. It suffices to verify (
7.5
) since, by
Lemma

6.5
, it implies that
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(r)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
is invertible. To that end, let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq[0,\mathfrak{T}]
denote a
N^{-20}
-mesh of
[0,\mathfrak{T}]
, and define the events

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{i}(s)=\bigcap_{j=\ell_{i}}^{m_{i}}\bigg\{|S_{jj}(s)|\geq(1+(\log N)^{-1})\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket\\
k\neq j\end{subarray}}|S_{jk}(s)|+(\log N)^{-1}\bigg\};\qquad\mathsf{F}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{\lfloor\mathfrak{T}\rfloor}\bigcap_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\mathsf{F}_{i}(s),

for any
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
and
s\in[0,\mathfrak{T}]
, where we have abbreviated the
(j,k)
entry of
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{\mathfrak{Q}}(s)}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
by
S_{jk}(s)
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{T}
, (
6.10
) holds for any
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
and
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
, so
Lemma

6.6
with a union bound yields that
\mathsf{F}
is overwhelmingly probable. Hence, we also restrict to
\mathsf{F}
.

For any
s\in[0,\mathfrak{T}]
, there exists an
s_{0}\in\mathcal{T}
with
|s-s_{0}|\leq N^{-20}
. Observe for
j,k\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
we have
|\mathfrak{l}(\Lambda_{j}-\Lambda_{k})|\leq 5(\log N)^{2}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}
) and

\displaystyle\big|\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}(s))-\chi^{\prime}(\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s_{0})-\mathfrak{Q}_{i}(s_{0}))\big|\leq 2B\mathfrak{M}^{-2}\cdot(\log N)^{3}\cdot|s-s_{0}|\leq N^{-10},

by the second statement in
Assumption

6.1
, the first statement in (
7.4
), and the facts that
B\leq T\leq N
and
|s-s_{0}|\leq N^{-20}
. Hence, we have for such
(j,k)
that
|S_{jk}(s)-S_{jk}(s_{0})|\leq N^{-5}
, by (
6.6
). Together with our restriction to
\mathsf{F}(s_{0})
, this confirms (
7.5
).
∎

We next show
Proposition

7.5
through a continuity argument, by verifying the following variant of (
7.4
), in which the second inequality there is replaced by a stronger one; we will prove it in
Section

7.3
below. For every
i\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, we denote
\mathfrak{T}_{i}=\min\{\mathfrak{T},i+1\}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

The following holds with overwhelming probability. For any indices
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
, and real number
t\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}-1]
, we have

(7.6)

\displaystyle|w_{j}(t)|\leq 3B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{19},

Proof of
Proposition

7.5
.

We first claim that the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any indices
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
, and real number
t\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}]
, we have
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2(\log N)^{2}
. To that end, fix
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
and, recalling
Definition

3.12
, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
, which we may by
Lemma

3.13
. Then, we have

(7.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,i]}|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2\log N\cdot\displaystyle\max_{\Lambda\in\bm{\Lambda}}|\Lambda|\leq 2(\log N)^{2},

where the first bound follows from (
7.1
) and the
U=\infty
case of
Lemma

6.5
(using (
7.5
) to verify its hypothesis (
6.7
) at
\varepsilon=(2\log N)^{-1}
); the second follows from our restriction to
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
.

Throughout the remainder of this proof, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
on which
Lemma

3.21
holds; the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
on which
Lemma

3.22
holds; to the event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
on which
Lemma

7.4
holds; to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
on which (
7.7
) holds; and to the event
\mathsf{E}_{5}
on which
Proposition

7.6
holds.

Now, fix
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
. It suffices to show that there exists some
\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}>\mathfrak{T}_{i}
for which
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
and
|w_{j}(t)|\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{20}
, as then this would contradict the maximality of
\mathfrak{T}
. For the former, in view of (
7.7
) (and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
), we have for each
t\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}]
that
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq 2(\log N)^{2}
. Observe that
\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}
is half-continuous at
t=\mathfrak{T}_{i}
, since by
Lemma

7.4
(and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
) the matrix
\bm{S}_{\bm{\Lambda};\bm{Q}(\mathfrak{T}_{i})}^{\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket}
satisfies (
7.5
) and is thus invertible. It follows that there exists some
\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}>\mathfrak{T}_{i}
such that
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(t)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
for each
t\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}]
, for sufficiently large
N
.

For the latter, we may assume that the above
\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{T}_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i}+1)
. It is quickly verified that
\varphi_{t}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{4},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{4}\rrbracket
for all
t\in[0,T]
, using the fact that
j\in\llbracket k_{1},k_{2}\rrbracket
, (
6.5
), and
Lemma

3.21
(with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
); thus,
Lemma

3.22
(with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
) applies with the
\varphi
there equal to
\varphi_{t}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))
here. Hence, for any
t\in[\mathfrak{T}_{i},\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}]
, we have

\displaystyle|w_{j}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq|w_{j}(\mathfrak{T}_{i}-1)|+(t-\mathfrak{T}_{i}+1)(\log N)^{3}+\big|q_{\varphi_{t}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))}(t)-q_{\varphi_{\mathfrak{T}_{i}-1}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))}(\mathfrak{T}_{i}-1)\big|

\displaystyle\leq 3B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{19}+2(\log N)^{3}+4(\log N)^{4}\leq B\mathfrak{M}^{1/2}(\log N)^{20},

which establishes the lemma. Here, in the first inequality we used the definitions (
7.3
) of
w_{j}
and (
1.15
) of
Q_{j}
, with the fact that
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}^{\prime}(s)|\leq(\log N)^{3}
for
s\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{\prime}]
; in the second we used (
7.6
) (with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{5}
), the fact that
t-\mathfrak{T}_{i}\leq 1
, and the second statement of
Lemma

3.22
(with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
); and in the third we used the
N
is sufficiently large.
∎

7.3.
Proof of
Proposition

7.6

In this section we establish
Proposition

7.6
. Throughout, we adopt the notation of
Section

7.2
and further fix an integer
i\in\llbracket 0,\mathfrak{T}\rrbracket
; an index
j\in\llbracket\ell_{i},m_{i}\rrbracket
; and a real number
t\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}-1]
. Additionally, recalling
Definition

3.12
, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(0)}(\log N)
throughout this proof, which we may by
Lemma

3.13
. We further restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{2}
on which
Lemma

3.11
holds; to the event
\mathsf{E}_{3}
on which
Lemma

3.21
holds; and to the event
\mathsf{E}_{4}
on which
Lemma

7.4
holds.

The following lemma indicates that
|Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}|
and
|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}|
are large if
|j-k|
is large. We will frequently use the fact that for sufficiently large
N
we have

(7.8)

\displaystyle\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{s^{\prime}}^{-1}(k))\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{6},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{6}\rrbracket,\quad\text{for any $k\in\llbracket k_{1},k_{2}\rrbracket$ and $s,s^{\prime}\in[0,T]$}.

as follows quickly from (
6.5
) and
Lemma

3.21
(with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
).

Lemma 7.7
.

Fix a real number
s\in[0,\mathfrak{T}_{i}]
and an index
k\in\llbracket k_{1},k_{2}\rrbracket
satisfying
|\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j))-\varphi_{s}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k))|\geq\mathfrak{M}(\log N)^{5}
. For
N
sufficiently large, we have

(7.9)

\displaystyle\big|Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(j)}(s)-Q_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}(s)\big|>3\mathfrak{M};\qquad|\mathfrak{Q}_{j}(s)-\mathfrak{Q}_{k}(s)|>2\mathfrak{M}.

Proof.

To establish the first bound in (
7.9
), observe that

(7.10)
