and
\varphi_{t}(j)
is an
N^{-2}\zeta
-localization center of
\tilde{\mu}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{M}}
. By
Lemma

3.19
(and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{7}
), there exists an index
\tilde{j}\in\llbracket 1,\tilde{N}\rrbracket
such that
|\tilde{\mu}-\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}}|\leq c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}}
and
\varphi_{t}(j)
is an
N^{-3}\zeta
-localization center of
\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}}
with respect to
\tilde{\bm{L}}(t)
. By
Lemma

3.22
(with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{4}
), we therefore have
|\varphi_{t}(j)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{4}
; similarly,
|\varphi_{0}(j)-\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{4}
. Combining the above estimates yields

(8.29)

\displaystyle|\lambda-\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}}|\leq 3c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}};\quad|\varphi_{0}(j)-\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{4};\quad|\varphi_{t}(j)-\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})|\leq(\log\tilde{N})^{4}.

By (
8.29
), (
8.27
), and (
8.28
), it follows that
N_{1}+T(\log\tilde{N})^{4}\leq\tilde{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{j})\leq N_{2}-T(\log\tilde{N})^{4}
for each
s\in\{0,t\}
. Thus, since
\llbracket N_{1}+3T(\log\tilde{N})^{4},N_{2}-3T(\log\tilde{N})^{4}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\tilde{N}_{1}+2T^{5},\tilde{N}_{2}-2T^{5}\rrbracket
(by (
8.23
)), (
8.26
) implies

(8.30)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big|\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})}(t)-\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})}(0)-tv_{\eff}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}})\big|\leq 30^{33}\cdot T^{1/2}(\log N)^{33}.

Observe by
Corollary

3.8
and (
8.29
) that

(8.31)

\displaystyle|tv_{\eff}(\lambda_{j})-tv_{\eff}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}})|\leq T\cdot(\log N)^{2}\cdot 3c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\log N)^{3}}\leq 1.

For any
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+K,N_{2}-K\rrbracket
and
s\in\{0,T\}
, further observe by (
8.25
) and (
1.9
) (with the fact that
a_{i}(s)\geq e^{-2(\log N)^{2}}
, by our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{6}
) that

(8.32)

\displaystyle|q_{i}(s)-\tilde{q}_{i}(s)|\leq 2N\cdot e^{-(\log N)^{3}}\cdot 2e^{2(\log N)^{2}}\leq 1.

Hence, for any
j\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{5},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{5}\rrbracket
, we have (as
(\log N)^{5}>3(\log\tilde{N})^{4}
, by (
8.23
))

\displaystyle\big|Q_{j}(t)-Q_{j}(0)-tv_{\eff}(\lambda_{j})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\tilde{Q}_{\tilde{j}}(t)-\tilde{Q}_{\tilde{j}}(0)-tv_{\eff}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}})\big|+|tv_{\eff}(\lambda_{j})-tv_{\eff}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\tilde{j}})|

\displaystyle\qquad+|q_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})}(0)-\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})}(0)|+|q_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})}(t)-\tilde{q}_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})}(t)|

\displaystyle\qquad+|q_{\varphi_{0}(j)}(0)-q_{\tilde{\varphi}_{0}(\tilde{j})}(0)|+|q_{\varphi_{t}(j)}(t)-q_{\tilde{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{j})}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq 30^{33}\cdot T^{1/2}(\log N)^{33}+3+2(\log N)^{5}\leq T^{1/2}(\log N)^{34}.

Here, in first bound, we used the definition (
1.15
) of
Q_{j}
. In the second, we used (
8.30
) to bound the first term, (
8.31
) to bound the second, (
8.32
) to bound the third and fourth, and (
3.3
) with (
8.29
) (using our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
) to bound the fifth and sixth. In the third bound, we used that
N
is sufficiently large. This establishes the theorem.
∎

Appendix A
Proofs of Results From
Section

3.1

A.1.
Proof of
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.2

In this section we establish
Lemma

3.1
and
Lemma

3.2
. We first establish the latter but, before doing so, we require the following lemma, indicating that the density
\varrho
is bounded (independently of
\theta
, though this will not be needed until the proof of
Lemma

3.9
in
Section

A.6
below).

Lemma A.1
.

There exists a constant
C=C(\beta)>1
(that is independent of
\theta>0
) such that
\varrho(x)<C
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Throughout, we adopt the notation from
Assumption

1.12
. By
Lemma

3.14
,
\varrho
is the limiting empirical spectral distribution of the random Lax matrix
\bm{L}
, sampled under thermal equilibrium. Due to this, the lemma follows from the Wegner estimate
[
2
, Theorem 4.1]
, whose hypotheses are verified by the fact (from
Definition

1.1
and
Definition

1.9
) that the density of any diagonal entry
L_{ii}
of
\bm{L}
, conditional on all of the other entries of
\bm{L}
, is bounded above (independently of
\theta
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
.

By
[
32
, Theorem 3.15]
, these exists a certain constant
Z_{\theta}>0
such that

(A.1)

\displaystyle\log\varrho_{\beta}(x)=2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)dy-\displaystyle\frac{\beta x^{2}}{2}-\log Z_{\theta},

and, by
[
32
, Section 3]
, this constant
Z_{\theta}
is explicitly given by

(A.2)

\displaystyle Z_{\theta}=\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}Z_{N;\theta/N}\cdot Z_{N-1;\theta/N}^{-1}

Here,
Z_{N;v}
is the partition function of the Gaussian
2v
Ensemble scaled by
\beta^{1/2}
, namely,

\displaystyle Z_{N;v}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\exp\bigg(-\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\lambda_{j}^{2}\bigg)\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{2v}\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}d\lambda_{j},

which is equal to (see
[
16
, Equation (2)]
)

(A.3)

\displaystyle Z_{N;v}=(2\pi)^{N/2}\beta^{-v\binom{N}{2}-N/2}\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma(1+vj)}{\Gamma(1+v)}.

Together, (
A.3
) and (
A.2
) yield
Z_{\theta}=(2\pi)^{1/2}\beta^{-\theta-1/2}\cdot\Gamma(\theta+1)
. By (
A.1
), this implies upon adding
\log\theta
to both sides that

\displaystyle\log(\theta\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(x))

\displaystyle=2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)dy+\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big((2\theta+1)\log\beta-\log(2\pi)-\beta x^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\log\Gamma(\theta+1)+\log\theta.

Differentiating both sides with respect to
\theta
yields

(A.4)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\varrho(x)}{\theta\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(x)}=2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho(y)dy+\log\beta-\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta+1)}{\Gamma(\theta+1)}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta},

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\Bigg(2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)dy\Bigg)=2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho(y)dy,

which holds since
\varrho=\partial_{\theta}(\theta\varrho_{\beta})
from
Definition

1.2
; since
\varrho
is bounded by
Lemma

A.1
; and since
\varrho
has subpolynomial tails by
Remark

3.15
.

The equality (
A.4
), with the fact that
\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta+1)=\Gamma(\theta)+\theta\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta)
(as
\Gamma(\theta+1)=\theta\cdot\Gamma(\theta)
), gives

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\varrho(x)}{\varrho_{\beta}(x)}=2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho(y)dy+\theta\log\beta-\theta\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta)}{\Gamma(\theta)}.

Together with the definitions (
1.13
) of
\bm{\mathrm{T}}
and (
1.10
) of
\alpha
, this establishes the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.1
.

The first two bounds in (
3.1
) are due to
[
18
, Lemma 2.2]
, so we must show the third. To that end, by
Lemma

3.2
and the first estimate in (
3.1
), it suffices to show that there exists a constant
C>1
such that
|\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)|\leq C(|x|+1)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. This follows from the fact that there exist constants
C_{1}>1
and
C_{2}>1
such that

\displaystyle|\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)|

\displaystyle=2\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho(y)dy\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\displaystyle\int_{x-1}^{x+1}\big|\log|x-y|\big|\varrho(y)dy+2(|x|+1)\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|>1}(|x|+1)^{-1}\cdot\big|\log|x-y|\big|\cdot\varrho(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}+2(|x|+1)\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|>1}(|x|+1)^{-1}\cdot\big|\log|x-y|\big|\cdot\varrho(y)dy\leq C_{1}+C_{2}(|x|+1),

where the first and second statements hold by (
1.13
), and the third holds by
Lemma

A.1
; and the fourth holds by
Remark

3.15
and the bound
(|x|+1)^{-1}\cdot|\log|x-y||\leq C_{3}(|y|+1)
for some constant
C_{3}>1
, whenever
|x-y|\geq 1
. ∎

A.2.
Proof of
Lemma

3.3

In this section we establish
Lemma

3.3
. This will proceed by expressing
\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)+\alpha
in terms of the resolvent of a random Lax matrix, and using known estimates on the latter. To implement the former, we require the following two lemmas; the first is a general expression for a certain entry of the resolvent of a tridiagonal matrix (essentially due to
[
42
]
, though we provide its quick proof here), and the second is a probabilistic interpretation for
\alpha
.

Lemma A.2

(
[
42
, Equation 3]
)
.

Fix integers
N_{1}\leq N_{2}
, and let
\bm{M}=[M_{ij}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
denote a symmetric, tridiagonal real matrix. For any
E\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\eig\bm{M}
, define
\bm{G}(E)=[G_{ij}(E)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
by
\bm{G}(E)=(\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id)^{-1}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle|G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)|=\displaystyle\prod_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}|M_{i,i+1}|\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{\mu\in\eig\bm{M}}|\mu-E|^{-1}.

Proof.

Set
N=N_{2}-N_{1}+1
. Let
\bm{C}(E)=[C_{ij}(E)]
denote the cofactor matrix of
\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id
, and observe that
G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)\cdot(\det\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id)^{-1}
. Since removing the row of index
N_{1}
and column of index
N_{2}
from
\bm{M}
yields an upper triangular
(N-1)\times(N-1)
matrix with diagonal entries
(M_{i,i+1})_{N_{1}\leq i<N_{2}}
, we deduce that
C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)=\prod_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}M_{i,i+1}
. Hence,

\displaystyle G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot C_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)\cdot(\det\bm{M}-E\cdot\Id)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N+1}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}M_{i,i+1}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{\mu\in\eig\bm{M}}(\mu-E)^{-1},

which confirms the lemma.
∎

Lemma A.3

(
[
1
, Lemma 3.11]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}>0
be a random variable with law
\mathbb{P}[\mathfrak{a}\in da]=2\beta^{\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}\cdot a^{2\theta-1}e^{-\beta a^{2}}da
. Denoting
\mathfrak{a}=e^{-\mathfrak{r}/2}
, we have that
\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{r}]=\alpha
.

We further require the following result indicating that the off-diagonal entries in the resolvent of a Lax matrix, of the Toda lattice under the thermal equilibrium, decay exponentially.

Lemma A.4

(
[
36
, Theorem 4]
)
.

Adopt
Assumption

1.12
. For any real number
s\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
c=c(s)>0
such that the following holds. For any
E\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\eig\bm{L}
, denote
\bm{G}(E)=[G_{ij}(E)]=(\bm{L}-E)^{-1}
. We have

(A.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{E\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\big[|G_{ij}(E)|^{s}\big]\leq c^{-1}e^{-c|i-j|}.

Now we can establish
Lemma

3.3
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
be a real number, and define the interval
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}=[x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon]
, so that
|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|=2\varepsilon
. Adopt
Assumption

1.12
and, for any
E\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\eig\bm{L}
, denote the resolvent
\bm{G}(E)=[G_{ij}(E)]=(\bm{L}-E)^{-1}
. Observe that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

(A.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -cN\geq\displaystyle\frac{4}{|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\log\mathbb{E}\big[|G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)|^{1/2}\big]dE&\geq\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\displaystyle\frac{2}{|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\log|G_{N_{1}N_{2}}(E)|dE\Bigg]\\
&=2\displaystyle\sum_{j=N_{1}}^{N_{2}-1}\mathbb{E}[\log L_{j,j+1}]-\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{2}{|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\log|\lambda_{i}-E|dE\Bigg],\end{aligned}

where the first statement follows from
Lemma

A.4
; the second from the concavity of
\log x
; and the third from
Lemma

A.2
. By
Assumption

1.12
,
Definition

1.9
, and
Definition

1.1
, if we denote
a_{j}=L_{j,j+1}
, then
a_{j}
has law
\mathbb{P}[a_{j}\in da]=2\beta^{\theta}\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}\cdot a^{2\theta-1}e^{-\beta a^{2}}da
. Hence, by
Lemma

A.3
, we have
2\cdot\mathbb{E}[\log L_{j,j+1}]=-\alpha
. Inserting this into (
A.6
) gives

(A.7)

\displaystyle\alpha+2\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}f(\lambda_{j})\Bigg]\geq c,\qquad\text{where}\qquad f(\lambda)=|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\log|\lambda-E|dE.

Next observe that there exists a constant
C=C(x,\varepsilon)>0
such that
|f(\lambda)|\leq C\log(\lambda^{2}+2)\leq C(\lambda^{2}+2)
. Therefore,
Lemma

3.14
and
Remark

3.15
together imply that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}f(\lambda_{j})\Bigg]=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda=|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|\lambda-E|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda dE.

Inserting this into (
A.7
) and letting
\varepsilon
tend to
0
gives

\displaystyle c\leq\alpha+\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\frac{2}{|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}|}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}}\log|\lambda-E|\varrho(\lambda)dEd\lambda=\alpha+2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-\lambda|\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda,

which upon recalling the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{T}}
from (
1.13
) yields the lemma.
∎

Remark A.5
.

The constant
c
in
Lemma

3.3
does not depend on
\theta
, as long as
\theta
is uniformly bounded. More precisely, for any fixed
\Theta>0
, the constant
c>0

Lemma

3.3
only depends on
\Theta
, as long as
\theta\in(0,\Theta)
. Indeed, it is quickly verified from the proof
[
36
, Theorem 4]
(see
[
36
, Equations (2.46) and (2.47)]
) that the constant
c
from
Lemma

A.4
only depends on the off-diagonal entries
(L_{ij})_{i\neq j}
of
\bm{L}
through a lower bound on
\mathbb{P}[|L_{ij}|\leq\tau]
, for some fixed
\tau>1
. Since this probability is uniformly bounded below independently of
\theta
, as long as
\theta\in(0,\Theta)
, the
c
from
Lemma

A.4
does not depend on
\theta\in(0,\Theta)
. Thus, the constant
c>0
in the proof of
Lemma

3.3
does not, either.

A.3.
Proofs of
Lemma

1.4
and
Lemma

1.5

In this section we show
Lemma

1.4
and
Lemma

1.5
. Define the function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and subspace
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
by for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
setting

(A.8)

\displaystyle\sigma(x)=\theta\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)+\theta\alpha;\qquad\mathcal{H}_{0}=\{f\in\mathcal{H}:\langle f,\varsigma_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}=0\},

and observe that
\sigma\in\mathcal{H}
. We begin by proving
Lemma

1.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

1.4
.

Fix
f\in\mathcal{H}
. Observe that there exists a constant
C>1
such that

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(y)f(y)dy\bigg|^{2}\varrho(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq 4\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(y)|^{2}\varrho(y)dy\cdot\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}\sigma(y)^{-1}\varrho_{\beta}(y)dy\Bigg|\cdot\varrho(x)dx\leq C\cdot\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},

where in the first statement we used (
1.11
); in the second we used the fact that
\varrho(x)=\sigma(x)\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(x)
by
Lemma

3.2
and (
A.8
); and in the third we used the facts that
\varrho_{\beta}
is bounded with subexponential decay (by
Lemma

3.1
) and
\sigma(y)>c
for some real number
c>0
(by
Lemma

3.3
and (
A.8
)), again with (
1.11
). This establishes the lemma.
∎

Next define
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=\theta^{-1}\bm{\sigma}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}
, whose domain is dense, as it contains any bounded, compactly supported function in
\mathcal{H}
. Thus,
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
is an unbounded, self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{H}
; denote its domain by
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. The following lemma essentially indicates that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

Lemma A.6
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, we have that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

Proof.

The lemma holds due to the sequence of equalities,

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\mathrm{S}}f,\varsigma_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sigma(x)f(x)\varrho(x)dx-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}f(x)\cdot\varrho(x)dx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)+\alpha)\cdot f(x)\varrho(x)dx-2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\cdot\varrho(y)f(y)\cdot\varrho(x)dydx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)+\alpha)\cdot f(x)\varrho(x)dx-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(y)\cdot f(y)\varrho(y)dy=\alpha\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)\varrho(x)dx=0.

Here, in the first statement we used (
1.11
) and the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
; in the second we used the definitions (
A.8
) of
\sigma
and (
1.13
) of
\bm{\mathrm{T}}
; in the third we interchanged the order of integration in the second integral and again used (
1.13
); and in the fourth and fifth we used that
f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
.
∎

The following (standard) lemma indicates that
\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}
is nonpositive on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
.

Lemma A.7
.

For any bounded, compactly supported function
f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, we have that
\langle\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}f,f\rangle_{\varrho}\leq 0
.

Proof.

Denoting
g(x)=f(x)\varrho(x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
, observe that

(A.9)

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho}f,f\rangle_{\varrho}=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{f(x)}\varrho(x)\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho}f(x)dx=2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{g(x)}g(y)\log|x-y|dxdy.

It suffices to show the right side of (
A.9
) is nonpositive, which will closely follow the proof of
[
4
, Lemma 2.6.2(d)]
. Observe in the below that
g
is bounded; compactly supported; and (as
f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
) satisfies
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(x)dx=0
.

To that end, observe for any real number
u\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
that

\displaystyle\log|u|=\displaystyle\int_{1}^{|u|}z^{-1}dz=\displaystyle\int_{1}^{|u|}(2z)^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-w/2}dwdz

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-2}\displaystyle\int_{1}^{|u|}ze^{-z^{2}/2t}dzdt

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-2}\displaystyle\int_{1}^{u^{2}}e^{-v/2t}dvdt

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(2t)^{-1}\cdot(e^{-1/2t}-e^{-u^{2}/2t})dt,

where the first two statements follow from performing the integration; the third from changing variables
w=t^{-1}z^{2}
; the fourth from changing variables
v=z^{2}
; and the fifth from performing the integration. Therefore,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\overline{g(x)}g(y)\log|x-y|dxdy=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(2t)^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(e^{-1/2t}-e^{-|x-y|^{2}/2t})\cdot\overline{g(x)}g(y)dxdydt,

where interchanging the order of integration is justified by the facts that
e^{-1/2t}-e^{-|x-y|^{2}/2t}
is of order
t^{-1}
as
t
tends to
\infty
and that it decays exponentially in
|x-y|^{2}/t
as
t
tends to
0
. Since
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}g(x)dx=0
, it follows that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\overline{g(x)}g(y)\log|x-y|dxdy

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(2t)^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-(x-y)^{2}/2t}\overline{g(x)}g(y)dxdy

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(8t\pi)^{-1/2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\mathrm{i}r(x-y)-tr^{2}/2}\overline{g(x)}g(y)drdxdydt

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(8t\pi)^{-1/2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-tr^{2}/2}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\mathrm{i}ry}g(y)dy\Bigg|^{2}drdt\leq 0,

which establishes the lemma.
∎

We next show that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
is a bijection from
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{H}
, from which we will deduce
Lemma

1.5
.

Corollary A.8
.

The operator
\bm{\mathrm{S}}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a bijection.

Proof.

We begin by observing that

(A.10)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{S}}\varsigma_{0}=\theta^{-1}\cdot\sigma(x)-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)=\alpha\cdot\varsigma_{0},

where the first statement holds by the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
, and the second from that (
A.8
) of
\sigma
. Hence,
\mathbb{C}\cdot\varsigma_{0}
is a one-dimensional eigenspace of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
that is not in its kernel, so
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
acts bijectively on it.

Therefore, it remains to show that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}
is a bijection, as
\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathbb{C}\cdot\varsigma_{0}
in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. By the spectral theorem for unbounded operators, namely,
[
37
, Proposition 5.13]
with
[
37
, Proposition 3.18]
, it suffices to show that there exists a constant
c>0
such that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}-c
is nonnegative as an operator on
\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. To that end, it suffices to show that
\langle(\bm{\mathrm{S}}-c)f,f\rangle_{\varrho}\geq 0
for any
f\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
, for
c>0
sufficiently small. We may assume that
f
is bounded and compactly supported, as such functions are dense in
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
. Then, we have for
c
sufficiently small that

\displaystyle\langle(\bm{\mathrm{S}}-c)f,f\rangle_{\varrho}=\theta^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sigma(x)-c\theta)\cdot|f(x)|^{2}\varrho(x)dx-\langle\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}f,f\rangle_{\varrho}\geq 0,

where the first statement follows from the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
, and the second from
Lemma

A.7
and the fact (by
Lemma

3.3
and the definition (
A.8
) of
\sigma
) that
\sigma(x)>\theta c
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

1.5
.

Observe by the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
, the definition (
A.8
) of
\sigma
, and
Lemma

3.2
that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})\bm{\sigma}
. Define
\mathcal{G}=\{\bm{\sigma}f:f\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\}
. Since
Lemma

3.3
yields a constant
c>0
such that
\sigma(x)>c
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, the multiplication operator
\bm{\sigma}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
is a bijection. Together with the fact from
Corollary

A.8
that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})\bm{\sigma}:\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a bijection, it follows that the operator
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}):\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a bijection. Hence, it admits an inverse
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})^{-1}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, which is a bijection. By
Lemma

1.4
, we have
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
, so to confirm that
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}):\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is a bijection it suffices to show that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
.

To that end, fix
g\in\mathcal{G}
, so that
g=\bm{\sigma}f
for some
f\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
and
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})g\in\mathcal{H}
. We must show that
g\in\mathcal{H}
, or equivalently that
\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}g\in\mathcal{H}
. Since
\varrho_{\beta}g=\varrho f
(as
\sigma(x)\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(x)=\varrho(x)
, by
Lemma

3.2
and (
A.8
)), this follows similarly to in the proof of
Lemma

1.4
, from the estimates

\displaystyle\|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\cdot\varrho(y)f(y)dy\bigg|^{2}\varrho(x)dx

\displaystyle\leq 4\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|f(y)|^{2}\varrho(y)dy\cdot\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}\varrho(y)dy\Bigg|\cdot\varrho(x)dx\leq C\cdot\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},

for some constant
C>1
, where we used the fact that
\varrho
is bounded and has subexponential decay in the last bound (from
Lemma

3.1
).
∎

Remark A.9
.

Observe by (
A.10
) and the fact that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})\bm{\sigma}
that
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})\sigma=0
if
\alpha=0
. Thus,
Lemma

1.5
is false if
\alpha=0
.

A.4.
Proofs of
Corollary

3.4
and
Lemma

3.5

In this section we establish
Corollary

3.4
and
Lemma

3.5
. We first require the following lemma.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{H}
be a function.

(1)

We have that
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}})^{-1}f=\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}f
.

(2)

If
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}f=0
, then
f=0
.

Proof.

The first statement of the lemma follows from the fact that
(\bm{g}-\bm{\varrho}_{\beta}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}=\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}(\bm{g}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}})
. The second follows from the fact that
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}=\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})
; the fact that
\varrho_{\beta}(x)\neq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
(by
Definition

1.2
); and the invertibility of
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}):\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, by
Lemma

1.5
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

3.4
.

The first statement of (
3.2
) implies the second, by
Lemma

3.2
, so it suffices to establish the former. To that end, observe by
Lemma

3.2
that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\theta^{-1}\cdot\varrho(x)-\varrho_{\beta}(x)\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)=\alpha\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(x),

or equivalently

(A.11)

\displaystyle(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\varrho=\alpha\cdot\varrho_{\beta}.

Further observe by the
f=\varsigma_{0}
case of the first statement of
Lemma

A.10
(and
Definition

1.6
) that
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}=\varrho_{\beta}
. Together with (
A.11
), this yields

\displaystyle(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\mathrm{T}})\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}(\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}-\alpha^{-1}\cdot\varrho_{\beta}^{-1}\cdot\varrho)=0.

By the second statement of
Lemma

A.10
, with the fact that
\varrho_{\beta}^{-1}\cdot\varrho\in\mathcal{H}
(which is a quick consequence of
Lemma

3.2
), this gives
\varrho=\alpha\cdot\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}\cdot\varrho_{\beta}
, yielding the first equality in (
3.2
) and thus the corollary.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.5
.

This follows from the equalities

\displaystyle(\theta^{-1}\cdot\bm{\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}}-\alpha^{-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho})v_{\eff}=\theta^{-1}\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}-\alpha^{-1}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho}\cdot v_{\eff}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\bm{\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}}\cdot v_{\eff}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}}\cdot\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}}\bm{\varrho_{\beta}})\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}=\varsigma_{1}.

Here, the first equality follows from the fact (by
Definition

1.8
) that
v_{\eff}=(\varsigma_{0}^{\dr})^{-1}\cdot\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}
; the second from the fact (by the first statement in (
3.2
)) that
\alpha^{-1}\cdot\varrho=\varrho_{\beta}\cdot\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}
; the third again from the fact that
v_{\eff}=(\varsigma_{0}^{\dr})^{-1}\cdot\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}
; and the fourth and fifth from the definition
Definition

1.6
of
\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}
.
∎

A.5.
Proof of
Lemma

3.6
,
Lemma

3.7
, and
Corollary

3.8

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

As in (
A.8
), denote
\sigma(x)=\theta\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}\varrho(x)+\theta\alpha
, which is positive and bounded away from
0
by
Lemma

3.3
. Further denote
g(x)=f^{\dr}(x)
and
h(x)=\sigma(x)^{-1}\cdot g(x)
. Since
(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})g=f
, we have

(A.12)

\displaystyle g(x)=\theta f(x)+2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)g(y)dy.

To bound the integral on the right side of (
A.12
), observe that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)g(y)dy\Bigg|\leq\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|h(y)|^{2}\varrho(y)dy\Bigg)^{1/2}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}\varrho(y)dy\Bigg)^{1/2},

where we have used the fact from
Lemma

3.2
that
\varrho(y)=\sigma(y)\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(y)
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
. By the boundedness and exponential decay of
\varrho
(from
Lemma

3.1
), there exists
C_{1}>1
such that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}\varrho(y)dy\Bigg|\leq C_{1}(\log(|x|+2))^{2},

from which we deduce

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\varrho_{\beta}(y)g(y)dy\Bigg|\leq C_{1}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\log(|x|+2).

Together with (
A.12
), this yields, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(A.13)

\displaystyle|g(x)-\theta f(x)|\leq 2\theta C_{1}\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\log(|x|+2).

We next bound
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}
. To do so, recall the operator
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=\theta^{-1}\bm{\sigma}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}
from
Section

A.3
. By
Lemma

3.2
(with the definition of
\sigma
), we have
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})\bm{\sigma}
, and so it follows since
h=\sigma^{-1}g=\sigma^{-1}f^{\dr}
that
\bm{\mathrm{S}}h=(\theta^{-1}-\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}})f^{\dr}=f
. Recalling the space
\mathcal{H}_{0}
from (
A.8
), denote
h=h_{0}+h_{1}
, where
h_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
h_{1}=\langle h,\varsigma_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}\cdot\varsigma_{0}\in\mathbb{C}\cdot\varsigma_{0}
. Then, for some
c\in(0,\alpha^{2})
, we have

(A.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=\|\bm{\mathrm{S}}h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}&=\|\bm{\mathrm{S}}h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\alpha^{2}\cdot\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\\
&\geq\langle\bm{\mathrm{S}}h_{0},h_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}^{2}\cdot\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{-2}+\alpha^{2}\cdot\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\\
&=\big(\theta^{-1}\langle\sigma h_{0},h_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}-\langle\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}h_{0},h_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}\big)^{2}\cdot\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{-2}+\alpha^{2}\cdot\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\\
&\geq\theta^{-2}\langle\sigma h_{0},h_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}^{2}\cdot\|h_{0}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{-2}+\alpha^{2}\cdot\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\\
&\geq c\cdot\langle h_{0},h_{0}\rangle_{\varrho}+\alpha^{2}\cdot\|h_{1}\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\geq c\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},\end{aligned}

where in the first statement we used the fact that
f=\bm{\mathrm{S}}h
; in the second we used the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathbb{C}\cdot\varsigma_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
, and from (
A.10
) that
\varsigma_{0}
is an eigenfunction of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
with eigenvalue
\alpha
; in the third and fourth we used the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}
; in the fifth we used the fact from
Lemma

A.7
that
\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho}
is nonpositive on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
; in the sixth we used the fact from
Lemma

3.3
that
\theta^{-1}\cdot\sigma(x)>c
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
; and in the seventh we again used that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is the orthogonal complement of
\mathbb{C}\cdot\varsigma_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
. Inserting (
A.14
) into (
A.13
) yields a constant
C_{2}>1
such that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(A.15)

\displaystyle|f^{\dr}(x)|=|g(x)|\leq C_{2}\cdot|f(x)|+C_{2}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\log(|x|+2),

proving the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.7
.

Denote
g(x)=f^{\dr}(x)
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, observe that

(A.16)

\displaystyle g^{\prime}=(f^{\prime}+\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g)^{\dr}.

Indeed, differentiating the relation
g(x)=\theta f(x)+\theta\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}g(x)
in
x
(and recalling (
1.13
)) yields

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)=\theta f^{\prime}(x)+2\theta\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|y|\cdot\partial_{x}\big(g(x-y)\varrho_{\beta}(x-y)\big)dy=\theta f^{\prime}+\theta\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}}g^{\prime}+\theta\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g,

from which we obtain (
A.16
). Applying
Lemma

3.6
, it follows there exists a constant
C_{1}>1
such that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(A.17)

\displaystyle|g^{\prime}(x)|\leq C_{1}\cdot(|f^{\prime}(x)|+|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g(x)|)+C_{1}\cdot(\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{H}}+\|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g\|_{\mathcal{H}})\cdot\log(|x|+2).

We must now bound
\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g
. To that end, observe from (
1.13
) that

(A.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g(x)|&\leq 2\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\log|x-y|\cdot\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}(y)|g(y)|dy\\
&\leq 2\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|g(y)|^{2}\varrho(y)dy\Bigg)^{1/2}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}\varrho(y)^{-1}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}(y)^{2}dy\Bigg)^{1/2}.\end{aligned}

From
Lemma

3.1
,
Lemma

3.2
, and
Lemma

3.3
, there exists constants
C_{2}>1
and
C_{3}>1
that

\displaystyle|\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}(y)|\leq C_{2}(|y|+1)\cdot\varrho_{\beta}(y)\leq C_{3}(|y|+1)\cdot\varrho(y).

Inserting this into (
A.18
) (and using the boundedness and exponential decay of
\varrho
, from
Lemma

3.1
) yields for some constants
C_{4}>1
and
C_{5}>1
that

\displaystyle|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g(x)|\leq C_{4}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\log|x-y|)^{2}(|y|+1)\varrho(y)dy\Bigg)^{1/2}\leq C_{5}\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\log(|x|+2).

By
Lemma

3.6
, we have
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{6}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}
for some
C_{6}>1
, so it follows for some
C_{7}>1
that

\displaystyle|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g(x)|\leq C_{7}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot\log(|x|+2);\qquad\|\bm{\mathrm{T}\varrho_{\beta}^{\prime}}g\|_{\mathcal{H}}\leq C_{7}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}.

Applying these bounds in (
A.17
) then yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

3.8
.

Fix
x\in[-A,A]
. Observe that there exists a constant
C_{1}>1
such that

\displaystyle|v_{\eff}(x)|\leq|\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}(x)|^{-1}\cdot|\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}(x)|\leq C_{1}(A+\log A)\leq 2C_{1}A,

where in the first statement we used
Definition

1.8
; in the second we used
Lemma

1.7
and
Lemma

3.6
; and in the third we used the fact that
A\geq 2
. This confirms the first statement of the corollary. Similarly, there exists a constant
C_{2}>1
such that

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}v_{\eff}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}(x)|^{-1}\cdot|\partial_{x}\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}(x)|+|\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}(x)|^{-2}\cdot|\varsigma_{1}^{\dr}(x)|\cdot|\partial_{x}\varsigma_{0}^{\dr}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\log A+C_{2}(\log A)^{2}+C_{2}A\log A\leq 2C_{2}A\log A,

where in the first statement we used
Definition

1.8
; in the second we used
Lemma

3.6
,
Lemma

3.7
, and
Lemma

1.7
; and in the third we used the fact that
A\geq 2
. This confirms the second statement of the corollary.
∎

A.6.
Proof of
Lemma

3.9

Proof of
Lemma

3.9
.

By
Lemma

A.1
and the explicit form of
\mathfrak{l}(x)=\log(x^{2}+\mathfrak{d}^{2})/2
, there exists a constant
C=C(\beta)>1
such that

(A.19)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\displaystyle\int_{\lambda-1}^{\lambda+1}|\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)|\varrho(x)dx\leq C.

Now, observe that
\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta)\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}
tends to
-\infty
as
\theta
tends to
0
. Therefore, there exists a constant
\theta_{0}=\theta_{0}(\beta)>0
such that, for any
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha=\log\beta-\Gamma^{\prime}(\theta)\cdot\Gamma(\theta)^{-1}>8C.

Together with (
A.19
), this implies for
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
that

\displaystyle 4\alpha^{-1}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\displaystyle\int_{\lambda-1}^{\lambda+1}|\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)|\varrho(x)dx<\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}.

Hence, since
\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)\geq 0
whenever
|x-\lambda|\geq 1
, it follows for
\theta\in(0,\theta_{0})
and any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)\varrho(x)dx+1\Bigg|

\displaystyle\geq 2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{|x-\lambda|>1}\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)\varrho(x)dx+1-2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{\lambda-1}^{\lambda+1}|\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)|\varrho(x)dx

\displaystyle>2\alpha^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathfrak{l}(x-\lambda)|\varrho(x)dx+\frac{1}{2},

thereby establishing the lemma.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of
Lemma

3.22

In this section we prove
Lemma

3.22
. We adopt the notation and assumptions of that lemma throughout. We further call a unit vector
\bm{v}=(v_{N_{1}},v_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,v_{N_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
nonnegatively normalized if
v_{j}>0
, where
j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
is the minimal index such that
v_{j}\neq 0
. For each index
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and real number
s\geq 0
, let
\bm{u}_{j}(s)=(u_{j}(N_{1};s),u_{j}(N_{1}+1;s),\ldots,u_{j}(N_{2};s))
denote the nonnegatively normalized, unit eigenvector of
\bm{L}(s)
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
.

We will use the following result showing approximate uniqueness for localization centers of
\bm{L}(t)
.

Lemma B.1

(
[
1
, Proposition 2.9]
)
.

Adopt
Assumption

1.12
, but assume more generally that
\zeta\geq N^{3}e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. The following holds with overwhelming probability. Fix
t\in[0,T]
;
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
; and
\zeta
-localization centers
\varphi,\tilde{\varphi}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
of
\lambda
with respect to
\bm{L}(t)
. If
N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3}\leq\varphi\leq N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}
, then
|\varphi-\tilde{\varphi}|\leq(\log N)^{3}
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.22
.

The proof of this lemma is similar to that of
[
1
, Lemma 5.2]
. Recalling
Definition

3.12
, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}=\bigcap_{s\geq 0}\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}(s)}(\log N)
, as we may by
Lemma

3.13
. We further restrict to the events
\mathsf{E}_{2}
on which
Lemma

3.11
holds and
\mathsf{E}_{3}
on which
Lemma

B.1
holds.

Next, we recall a fact concerning the evolution of the Lax matrix
\bm{L}(s)
. For each
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define the tridiagonal skew-symmetric matrix
\bm{P}(s)=[P_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
as follows. For each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
, set
P_{i,i+1}(s)=a_{i}(s)/2
and
P_{i+1,i}(s)=-a_{i}(s)/2
; for all
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket^{2}
not of the above form, set
P_{i,j}(s)=0
. For any real number
s\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, further let
\bm{V}(s)=[V_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
satisfy the ordinary differential equation
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(s)
, with initial data
\bm{V}(0)=\Id
; the existence of such a matrix
\bm{V}(s)
follows from the Picard–Lindelöf theorem. Similarly, fixing
r\geq 0
, for any real number
s\geq r
, let
\bm{V}(r;s)=[\bm{V}(r;s)]\in\Mat_{\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket}
satisfy the ordinary differential equation
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(r;s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(r;s)
, with initial data
\bm{V}(r;r)=\bm{V}(r)
. Observe that
\bm{V}(0;r)=\bm{V}(r)
. For any
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket^{2}
, the
(i,j)
-entry of
\bm{V}(r;s)
is more explicitly given by

(B.1)

\displaystyle V_{ij}(r;s)=V_{ij}(r)+\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\cdots\displaystyle\sum_{i_{k}=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\displaystyle\int_{r}^{s}\cdots\displaystyle\int_{r}^{s}\mathbbm{1}_{s_{1}>s_{2}>\cdots>s_{k}}\cdot V_{i_{k}j}(r)\displaystyle\prod_{h=1}^{k}P_{i_{h-1},i_{h}}(s_{h})ds_{h},

where we have set
i_{0}=i
. Indeed, it is quickly verified under this choice that
\bm{V}(r;r)=\bm{V}(r)
and
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(r;s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(r;s)
, the latter by differentiating both sides of (
B.1
) with respect to
s
. Then, by
[
31
, Section 2]
, we have
\bm{V}(r;s)^{-1}\cdot\bm{L}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(r;s)=\bm{L}(r)
.

This implies that
\bm{L}(s)=\bm{V}(r;s)\cdot\bm{L}(r)\cdot\bm{V}(r;s)^{\mathsf{T}}
, as
\bm{V}(r;s)
is orthogonal (since
\bm{V}(0)=\Id
,
\partial_{s}\bm{V}(r;s)=\bm{P}(s)\cdot\bm{V}(r;s)
, and
\bm{P}(s)
is skew-symmetric). Hence, letting
\bm{U}(s)=[U_{ij}(s)]\in\Mat_{N\times N}
denote matrix of eigenvectors of
\bm{L}(s)
, whose
(i,j)
-entry is given by
U_{ij}(s)=u_{j}(i;s)
for each
(i,j)\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, we have
\bm{U}(s)=\bm{V}(r;s)\cdot\bm{U}(r)
. In particular,

(B.2)

\displaystyle u_{j}(i;s)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}V_{ik}(r;s)\cdot u_{j}(k;r).

Now observe whenever
|i-j|\geq 20(s-r)\log N
that

(B.3)

\displaystyle|V_{ij}(r;s)|\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{(s-r)^{k}}{k!}\cdot(2\log N)^{k}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{2e(s-r)\log N}{k}\bigg)^{k}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=|i-j|}^{\infty}e^{-k}\leq 2e^{-|i-j|}.

Here, in the first inequality we used (
B.1
), with the facts that each
|P_{ij}(s_{h})|\leq\log N
(as we have restricted to the event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
), that
P_{ij}=0
whenever
|i-j|\neq 1
(meaning that there are at most two choices for each
i_{h}
that gives rise to a nonzero summand in (
B.1
)), and that
|V_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(r)|\leq 1
for all
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
(as
\bm{V}(r)
is orthogonal); in the second we used the bound
k!\geq(e^{-1}k)^{k}
for each
k\geq 0
; in the third we used the bound
2k^{-1}e(s-r)\log N\leq e^{-1}
for
k\geq|i-j|\geq 20(s-r)\log N
; and in the fourth we performed the sum.

Now we can establish the lemma. We assume that
t^{\prime}\geq t
, as the proof when
t^{\prime}<t
is entirely analogous. Let
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
be such that
\lambda=\lambda_{j}
, and denote
\Delta=(|t-t^{\prime}|+1)(\log N)^{3}
and
\zeta_{0}=N^{3}e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
. Assuming to the contrary that
|\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}|>(|t-t^{\prime}|+2)(\log N)^{3}=\Delta+(\log N)^{3}
, the first statement of the lemma follows from the bounds

\displaystyle|u_{j}(\varphi^{\prime};t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-\varphi^{\prime}|\geq\Delta}\cdot|V_{\varphi^{\prime}k}(t;t^{\prime})|+\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-\varphi|>(\log N)^{3}}\cdot|u_{j}(k;t)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\displaystyle\sum_{k=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|k-\varphi^{\prime}|\geq\Delta}\cdot e^{-|k-\varphi^{\prime}|}+N\zeta_{0}\leq N^{5}e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}<\zeta,

which contradicts the fact that
\varphi^{\prime}
is a
\zeta
-localization center of
\bm{u}_{j}(t^{\prime})
. Here, the first inequality follows from (
B.2
), together with the facts that
|u_{j}(k;0)|\leq 1
(as
\bm{u}_{j}
is a unit vector) and
|V_{mk}(t;t^{\prime})|\leq 1
(as
\bm{V}(t;t^{\prime})
is orthogonal); the second follows from (
B.3
) (with the fact that
\Delta>20(t^{\prime}-t)\log N
for sufficiently large
N
) and the fact from
Lemma

B.1
(and our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{3}
) that
k
is not a
\zeta_{0}
-localization center for
\bm{u}_{j}(t)
if
|k-\varphi|>(\log N)^{3}
; and the third and fourth follow from performing the sum and recalling that
\Delta\geq(\log N)^{3}
, that
\zeta_{0}=N^{3}e^{-200(\log N)^{3/2}}
, and that
\zeta\geq e^{-150(\log N)^{3/2}}
.

Therefore, since
\varphi\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{4},N_{1}-T(\log N)^{4}\rrbracket
, we have
\varphi^{\prime}\in\llbracket N_{1}+T(\log N)^{3},N_{2}-T(\log N)^{3}\rrbracket
, so
Lemma

3.11
applies with the
(i,j)
there equal to
(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime})
here. The second statement of the lemma then follows from the estimates

\displaystyle|q_{\varphi}(t)-q_{\varphi^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|q_{\varphi}(t)-q_{\varphi}(t^{\prime})|+|q_{\varphi}(t^{\prime})-q_{\varphi^{\prime}}(t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq|t-t^{\prime}|\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|b_{\varphi}(s)|+\alpha\cdot|\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}|+|\varphi-\varphi^{\prime}|^{1/2}(\log N)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|t-t^{\prime}|\cdot\log N+2\alpha(|t-t^{\prime}|+2)(\log N)^{7/2}\leq(|t-t^{\prime}|+1)(\log N)^{4},

where the second inequality holds by (
1.5
), (
1.7
), and (
3.3
) (with our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{2}
); the third holds from our restriction to
\mathsf{E}_{1}
and the first part of the lemma; and the fourth holds since
N
is sufficiently large. ∎

Appendix C
Proof of
Lemma

5.2

In this section we establish
Lemma

5.2
. Setting
|\mathcal{I}|=n
, we assume throughout that
m=n
and
\mathcal{J}_{i}=\{i\}
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, as the proof is entirely analogous in the general case. Further set
A_{k}=\Infl_{x_{k}}(F;p)
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. We begin with the following lemma that exhibits a set
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
that
\bm{x}
is likely to lie in, on which
F
changes by a bounded amount upon perturbing a given coordinate. In what follows, for any integer
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and
n
-tuple
\bm{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\bm{w}^{(k)}=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{k-1},w_{k+1},\ldots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
denote the
(n-1)
-tuple obtained by removing
w_{k}
from
\bm{w}
.

Lemma C.1
.

There exists a subset
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\in\mathcal{Y}]\geq 1-2np^{1/2}
, such that for each
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle|F(\bm{u})-F(\bm{v})|\leq A_{k},\qquad\text{for any $\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathcal{Y}$ with $\bm{u}^{(k)}=\bm{v}^{(k)}$}.

Proof.

Fix
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Define the
n
-tuple
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
of mutually independent random variables, by setting
\bm{y}^{(k)}=\bm{x}^{(k)}
, and setting
y_{k}
to be a random variable with the same law as
x_{k}
that is independent from
\bm{x}\cup\bm{y}^{(k)}
. Observe by
Definition

5.1
that
\mathbb{P}[|F(\bm{x})-F(\bm{y})|\geq A_{k}]\leq p
. Therefore, a Markov bound yields subsets
\mathcal{Y}_{k,1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{k,2}(\bm{w}^{(k)})\subseteq\mathbb{R}
for each
\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k,1}
, satisfying the following two properties. First, for each
\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k,1}
, we have

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}^{(k)}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{k,1}]\geq 1-p^{1/2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{P}[x_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{k,2}(\bm{w}^{(k)})]\geq 1-p^{1/2}.

Second, denoting
\mathcal{Y}_{k}=\{\bm{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k,1},w_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k,2}(\bm{w}^{(k)})\}
, we have

(C.2)

\displaystyle|F(\bm{u})-F(\bm{v})|\leq A_{k},\qquad\text{for any $\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k}$ such that $\bm{u}^{(k)}=\bm{v}^{(k)}$}.

Now set
\mathcal{Y}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{Y}_{k}
. By (
C.1
) and the independence between
\bm{x}^{(k)}
and
x_{k}
, we have that
\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\in\mathcal{Y}_{k}]\geq(1-p^{1/2})^{2}\geq 1-2p^{1/2}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Hence, a union bound yields
\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\in\mathcal{Y}]\geq 1-2np^{1/2}
, which verifies the first estimate in the lemma. The second follows from (
C.2
).
∎

Now we can establish
Lemma

5.2
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
denote the set from
Lemma

C.1
, and define the function
G:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
G(\bm{x})=F(\bm{x})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{Y}}
, for each
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then, for any
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\bm{u}^{(j)}=\bm{v}^{(j)}
, we have
(G(\bm{u})-G(\bm{v}))^{2}\leq A_{j}^{2}
, by
Lemma

C.1
. Hence,
[
8
, Theorem 12]
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\big|G(\bm{x})-\mathbb{E}[G(\bm{x})]\big|\geq RS^{1/2}\big]\leq 2e^{-R^{2}/4}.

Moreover, by
Lemma

C.1
, we have
\mathbb{P}[F(\bm{x})\neq G(\bm{x})]\leq 2np^{1/2}
, and so it follows that

(C.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\big|F(\bm{x})-\mathbb{E}[G(\bm{x})]\big|\geq RS^{1/2}\big]\leq 2np^{1/2}+2e^{-R^{2}/4}.

Additionally, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}[G(\bm{x})-F(\bm{x})]\big|\leq\mathbb{E}[|F(\bm{x})|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\bm{x}\notin\mathcal{Y}}]\leq\mathbb{P}[\bm{x}\notin\mathcal{Y}]^{1/2}\cdot\mathbb{E}[F(\bm{x})^{2}]^{1/2}\leq U(2np^{1/2})^{1/2},

where we used the definition of
G
,
Lemma

C.1
, and the definition of
U
from (
5.1
). Upon insertion into (
C.3
), this yields the lemma. ∎

Appendix D
Proof of
Lemma

5.7

In this section we prove
Lemma

5.7
; we adopt the notation of
Section

5.4
throughout.

An approximation of the linear functional
\sum_{i=1}^{N}H(\lambda_{i})
of
\bm{L}
is provided by
Lemma

3.14
, but without an effective error. To remedy this, we will “embed”
\bm{L}
into a much larger matrix
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
; compare expectations of their linear functionals; and apply
Lemma

3.14
to
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
. In what follows, we abbreviate
\bm{a}(0)=\bm{a}=(a_{N_{1}},a_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,a_{N_{2}-1})
and
\bm{b}(0)=\bm{b}=(b_{N_{1}},b_{N_{1}+1},\ldots,b_{N_{2}})
.

Let
\mathfrak{N}_{1}<\mathfrak{N}_{2}
be integers with
\mathfrak{N}_{1}<N_{1}
and
N_{2}<\mathfrak{N}_{2}
. Set
\mathfrak{N}=\mathfrak{N}_{2}-\mathfrak{N}_{1}+1
; we will take
\mathfrak{N}
sufficiently large so that (
D.1
) below holds. Sample
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}};\bm{\mathfrak{b}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{N}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{N}}
according to the thermal equilibrium
\mu_{\beta,\theta;\mathfrak{N}-1,\mathfrak{N}}
(from
Definition

1.1
), and denote
\bm{\mathfrak{a}}=(\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{N}_{1}},\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{N}_{1}+1},\ldots,\mathfrak{a}_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}-1})
and
\bm{b}=(\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{N}_{1}},\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{N}_{1}+1},\ldots,\mathfrak{b}_{\mathfrak{N}_{2}})
; we couple
(\bm{\mathfrak{a}};\bm{\mathfrak{b}})
with
(\bm{a};\bm{b})
so that
\mathfrak{a}_{i}=a_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}-1\rrbracket
and
\mathfrak{b}_{i}=b_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
. Define
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}=[\mathfrak{L}_{ik}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}\rrbracket}
(as in
Definition

1.9
) by setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,i}=\mathfrak{b}_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}\rrbracket
; setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,i+1}=\mathfrak{L}_{i+1,i}=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
for each
i\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}-1\rrbracket
; and setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,k}=0
for all
(i,k)\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}\rrbracket^{2}
not of the above form. By
Lemma

3.14
, we may choose
\mathfrak{N}
sufficiently large (relative to
N
and
H
) so that

(D.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}}\cdot\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}H(\nu)-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\bigg|\geq\displaystyle\frac{A}{N}\Bigg]\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}};\\
&\mathbb{P}\big[\#\{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}:|\nu|\geq\log N\}\geq N^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{N}\big]\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}},\end{aligned}

where in the last bound we used the superexponential decay of
\varrho
from
Lemma

3.1
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote the resolvents
\bm{G}(z)=[G_{ik}(z)]=(\bm{L}-z)^{-1}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}(z)=[\mathfrak{G}_{ik}(z)]=(\bm{\mathfrak{L}}-z)^{-1}
. The following lemma compares the diagonal entries of
\bm{G}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}
.

Lemma D.1
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Set
\eta=e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
, and define
\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-N\leq\Real z\leq N,\eta\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
. Then, for any complex number
z\in\Omega
and integer
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+(\log N)^{4},N_{2}-(\log N)^{4}\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(z)-G_{ii}(z)|\leq c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{4}}.

The proof of
Lemma

D.1
will make use of the below estimate on resolvents of perturbations of random Lax matrices, as in the context of
Assumption

3.18
.

Lemma D.2

(
[
1
, Lemma 5.4]
)
.

There exists a constant
c\in(0,1)
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Adopt
Assumption

3.18
and, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote
\bm{G}(z)=[G_{ij}(z)]=(\bm{L}-z)^{-1}
and
\tilde{\bm{G}}(z)=[\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)]=(\tilde{\bm{L}}-z)^{-1}
. Let
\eta\in[\delta,1]
be a real number; and define the set
\Omega=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-N\leq\Real z\leq N,\eta\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
. For any
i,j\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, we have

(D.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\Omega}|G_{ij}(z)-\tilde{G}_{ij}(z)|\leq e^{(\log N)^{2}}\eta^{-2}(\delta^{1/4}+e^{-c\dist(i,\mathcal{D})-c\dist(j,\mathcal{D})}).

Proof of
Lemma

D.1
.

This lemma would follow from
Lemma

D.2
except for the fact that, if it were used directly, the
N
there must be
\mathfrak{N}
here, and this would cause the prefactor
e^{(\log\mathfrak{N})^{2}}
in (
D.2
) to be too large. To circumvent this, we use
Lemma

D.2
inductively.

Fix an integer
r\geq 1
and a triple of integers
(\mathfrak{N}_{0;m},\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m})
for each
m\in\llbracket 0,r\rrbracket
, satisfying the following properties. First, we have that
\mathfrak{N}_{0;m}=\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}-\mathfrak{N}_{1;m}+1\geq 1
and
r\leq\log\mathfrak{N}
. Second, we have
(\mathfrak{N}_{0;0},\mathfrak{N}_{1;0},\mathfrak{N}_{2;0})=(\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2})
and
(\mathfrak{N}_{0;r},\mathfrak{N}_{1;r},\mathfrak{N}_{2;r})=(N,N_{1},N_{2})
. Third, for each
m\in\llbracket 0,r-1\rrbracket
, we have

(D.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{1;m}<\mathfrak{N}_{1;m+1}<\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{N}_{1;m}}{10}<0<\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}}{10}<\mathfrak{N}_{2;m+1}<\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}.

It is quickly verified that such integers exist, since
\mathfrak{N}_{1}<N_{1}<0<N_{2}<\mathfrak{N}_{2}
.

For each
m\in\llbracket 0,r\rrbracket
, define
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{m}=[\mathfrak{L}_{i,k;m}]\in\SymMat_{\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket}
(as in
Definition

1.9
) by setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,i;m}=\mathfrak{b}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket
; setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,i+1;m}=\mathfrak{L}_{i+1,i;m}=\mathfrak{a}_{i}
for
i\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}-1\rrbracket
; and setting
\mathfrak{L}_{i,k;m}=0
for
(i,k)\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket^{2}
not of the above form. For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
, denote
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}_{m}(z)=[\mathfrak{G}_{i,k;m}(z)]=(\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{m}-z)^{-1}
. Then,
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{0}=\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
;
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{r}=\bm{L}
;
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}(z)=\bm{\mathfrak{G}}(z)
; and
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}_{r}(z)=\bm{G}(z)
.

For each
m\in\llbracket 0,r\rrbracket
, define the set
\Omega_{m}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:-\mathfrak{N}_{0;m}\leq\Real z\leq\mathfrak{N}_{0;m},\eta\leq\Imaginary z\leq 1\}
. We next apply
Lemma

D.2
, with the
(N,\delta;\bm{L})
there equal to
(\mathfrak{N}_{m-1};0;\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{m-1})
here, and the
\tilde{\bm{L}}
there given by the extension by
0
of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}_{m}
to
\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m-1},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m-1}\rrbracket
here, meaning that we set
\mathfrak{L}_{i,k;m}=0
if
i
or
k
is in
\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m-1},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m-1}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket
; observe that
Lemma

3.13
verifies the assumption (
3.7
), with the
\mathcal{D}
there equal to
\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m-1},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m-1}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket
here. In this way, the
\Omega
,
G_{ii}(z)
, and
\tilde{G}_{ii}(z)
from
Lemma

D.2
become
\Omega_{m}
,
\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m-1}(z)
, and
\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m}(z)
here, respectively. Thus, there exists a constant
c_{1}>0
and an event
\mathsf{G}_{m}
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{G}_{m}^{\complement}]\leq c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{2}}
, such that the following holds on
\mathsf{G}_{m}
. For any complex number
z\in\Omega_{m}
and integer
i\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m}+(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{4},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}-(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{4}\rrbracket
(meaning that
\dist(i,\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m-1},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m-1}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1;m},\mathfrak{N}_{2;m}\rrbracket)\geq(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{4}
), we have

(D.4)

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m-1}(z)-\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m}(z)|\leq c_{1}^{-1}\eta^{-2}\cdot e^{(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m-1})^{2}-c_{1}(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{4}}.

Thus, denoting
\mathsf{G}=\bigcap_{m=1}^{r}\mathsf{G}_{m}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{G}^{\complement}]\leq\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{G}_{m}^{\complement}]\leq c_{1}^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{r}e^{-c_{1}(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{2}}\leq 2c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(\log N)^{2}/2},

where the last inequality holds by (
D.3
). Moreover, by summing the estimates (
D.4
), we deduce that there exists a constant
c>0
so that, for all
z\in\bigcap_{m=1}^{r}\Omega_{m}=\Omega
and
i\in\llbracket N_{1}+(\log N)^{4},N_{2}-(\log N)^{4}\rrbracket
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(z)-G_{ii}(z)|\leq\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}|\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m-1}(z)-\mathfrak{G}_{ii;m}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}^{-1}\eta^{-2}\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{r}e^{(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m-1})^{2}-c_{1}(\log\mathfrak{N}_{0;m})^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{4}},

where the last inequality again holds by (
D.3
) (with the fact that
\eta=e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
). This establishes the lemma.
∎

We next have the following lemma, expressing linear functionals of
\bm{L}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
through their resolvent entries. We then deduce
Lemma

5.7
as a consequence.

Lemma D.3
.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that the following holds with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. Letting
n_{1}^{\prime}=n_{1}+(\log N)^{5}
;
n_{2}^{\prime}=n_{2}-(\log N)^{5}
;
\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{N}_{1}+(\log\mathfrak{N})^{5}
;
\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{N}_{2}-(\log\mathfrak{N})^{5}
; and
\eta=e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
, we have

(D.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\leq 6A(\log N)^{5};\\
&\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{N}{\mathfrak{N}}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}H(\nu)-\displaystyle\frac{N}{\mathfrak{N}\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\leq 7A(\log N)^{5}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

Define the function
\tilde{H}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
\tilde{H}(\lambda)=H(\lambda)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|\lambda|\leq\log N}
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Throughout, we restrict to the event
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
, which we may do by
Lemma

3.13
. Then we claim that, with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
,

(D.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{i})-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi n}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{6A}{n}\cdot(\log N)^{5};\\
&\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{N}}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}\tilde{H}(\nu)-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi\mathfrak{N}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{6A}{\mathfrak{N}}\cdot(\log\mathfrak{N})^{5}.\end{aligned}

Then the first bound in (
D.6
) yields the first statement in (
D.5
), since
H(\lambda)=\tilde{H}(\lambda)
for each
\lambda\in\eig\bm{L}
(by our restriction to
\mathsf{BND}_{\bm{L}}(\log N)
); since
H(E)=\tilde{H}(E)
whenever
|E|\leq\log N
; and since
\tilde{H}(E)=0
whenever
|E|>\log N
. Moreover, multiplying the second bound in (
D.6
) by
N
and using the facts that
N\leq\mathfrak{N}
; that
\tilde{H}(\nu)\neq H(\nu)
only if
|\nu|\geq\log N
; that
|\tilde{H}(\nu)|\leq A
for such
\nu
; and that there are at most
N^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{N}
many such
\nu
with probability at least
1-e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
(by the second bound in (
D.1
)), we deduce the second statement in (
D.5
).

Therefore, it suffices to verify (
D.6
). The proofs of both bounds there are entirely analogous (observe if
(n_{1},n_{2})=(N_{1},N_{2})
then the second bound of (
D.6
) takes a similar form to the first one, with the
\bm{L}
there replaced by
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
), so we only show the former. To that end, first observe that

\displaystyle G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{u_{k}(i)^{2}}{\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta},

for any
i\in\llbracket N_{1},N_{2}\rrbracket
, and so

(D.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE.\end{aligned}

It will next be useful to restrict the sum over
k
on the right side of (
D.7
). To do so, we have by
[
1
, Equation (5.1)]
and a union bound that there exists a constant
c>0
and an event
\mathsf{G}
with
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{G}^{\complement}]\leq c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
such that, on
\mathsf{G}
, we have

(D.8)

\displaystyle|u_{k}(i)|\leq e^{-c(\log N)^{5}},\qquad\text{whenever $|i-\varphi_{0}(k)|\geq(\log N)^{5}$}.

We restrict to
\mathsf{G}
in what follows. Then, denoting
\mathcal{K}=\{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(m):m\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}&\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\Imaginary(\Lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&=\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\\
&\qquad-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{i=N_{1}}^{N_{2}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{k\notin\mathcal{K}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&\qquad+\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{i\notin\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|,\end{aligned}

where we used the orthonormality of the
(\bm{u}_{k})
, the fact that
\Lambda_{k}=\lambda_{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(k)}
, and the definition of
\mathcal{K}
. Setting
n_{1}^{\prime\prime}=n_{1}-(\log N)^{5}
and
n_{2}^{\prime\prime}=n_{2}+(\log N)^{5}
, it follows that

(D.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}&\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE-\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\Imaginary(\Lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime\prime},n_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&\qquad+N^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\tilde{H}(E)|\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\displaystyle\max_{k:|i-\varphi_{0}(k)|\geq(\log N)^{5}}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE,\end{aligned}

where we used the fact that
|i-\varphi_{0}(k)|\geq(\log N)^{5}
if
i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and
k\notin\mathcal{K}
, or if
k\in\mathcal{K}
and
i\notin\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime\prime},n_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket
(by the definition of
\mathcal{K}
). Moreover,

(D.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} N^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\tilde{H}(E)|\cdot\displaystyle\max_{i\in\llbracket N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}\displaystyle\max_{k:|i-\varphi_{0}(k)|\geq(\log N)^{5}}&u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\\
&\leq 2AN^{3}\eta^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{5}}\leq Ae^{-(\log N)^{3}},\end{aligned}

where in the first statement we used the facts that
\supp\tilde{H}\subseteq[-\log N,\log N]\subseteq[-N,N]
, that
|\tilde{H}(E)|\leq A
for all
E\in\mathbb{R}
, and that (
D.8
) holds (by our restriction to
\mathsf{G}
); in the second, we used the fact that
\eta=e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
. Additionally, we have

(D.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime\prime},n_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket}u_{k}(i)^{2}\cdot\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq 4A(\log N)^{5}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Imaginary(\lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE=4\pi A(\log N)^{5},\end{aligned}

where in the first statement we used the facts that
|\tilde{H}(E)|\leq A
for each
E\in\mathbb{R}
, that there are most
4(\log N)^{5}
indices
i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime\prime},n_{2}^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\setminus\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, and that the
\bm{u}_{k}
are orthonormal; in the second, we used the fact that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Imaginary(\Lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE=\pi
.

Combining (
D.7
), (
D.9
), (
D.10
), and (
D.11
), we deduce that

(D.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{i})-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\\
&\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{i})-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{H}(E)\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary(\Lambda_{k}-E-\mathrm{i}\eta)^{-1}dE\Bigg|+5A(\log N)^{5}\\
&=\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{(\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{i})-\tilde{H}(E))dE}{(\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\Bigg|+5A(\log N)^{5},\end{aligned}

where in the last statement we used the identity
\eta\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}((\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2})^{-1}dE=\pi
for each
k\in\llbracket n_{1},n_{2}\rrbracket
. Now, denoting
\eta^{\prime}=e^{-(\log N)^{5/2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{(\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{k})-\tilde{H}(E))dE}{(\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{\pi}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{|E-\Lambda_{k}|\leq\eta^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{|\tilde{H}(\Lambda_{k})-\tilde{H}(E)|}{(\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2}}+2A\displaystyle\int_{|E-\Lambda_{k}|>\eta^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{dE}{(\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{\pi}\Bigg(Ae^{-(\log N)^{2}}\displaystyle\int_{|E-\Lambda_{k}|\leq\eta^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{dE}{(\Lambda_{k}-E)^{2}+\eta^{2}}+4A\eta^{\prime-1}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq AN(e^{-(\log N)^{2}}+4\eta^{\prime-1}\eta)\leq 5ANe^{-(\log N)^{2}},

where in the first bound we used the fact that
|\tilde{H}(\Lambda)|\leq A
for all
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
; in the second we used (
5.16
); in the third we evaluated the integral; and in the fourth we used the fact that
\eta^{\prime-1}\eta\leq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
. This, with (
D.12
), verifies the first bound in (
D.6
). As mentioned above, the second is shown analogously; this establishes the lemma.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

5.7
.

We adopt the notation for the parameters
\eta=e^{-(\log N)^{3}}
,
(n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime})
, and
(\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime})
from
Lemma

D.3
throughout, and also set
n^{\prime}=n_{2}^{\prime}-n_{1}^{\prime}+1
and
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}-\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}+1
. We may assume that
(\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2})
are such that there exists an integer
\mathfrak{K}\geq 1
such that
\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{K}\cdot n^{\prime}
.

Let us first show for any indices
i_{1},i_{2}\in\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
with
i_{2}-i_{1}+1=n^{\prime}
that

(D.13)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]\Bigg|\leq A.

Denote
I=i_{1}-n_{1}=i_{2}-n_{2}
(where the last equality holds since
i_{2}-i_{1}=n^{\prime}-1=n_{2}-n_{1})
. By
Lemma

D.1
(shifting the row and column indices of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
there by
I
), there exists a constant
c>0
such that we can couple
\bm{G}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{G}}
such that the following holds. With probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
, we have
|G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)-\mathfrak{G}_{i+I,i+I}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)|\leq c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
for each
i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and
E\in[-N,N]
. Summing over
i\in\llbracket n_{1}^{\prime},n_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
; multiplying by
H(E)
; using the fact that
|H(E)|\leq A
for all
E\in\mathbb{R}
; and integrating over
E\in[-\log N,\log N]
then gives with probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
that

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=i_{1}}^{i_{2}}\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE-\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\Bigg|\leq c^{-1}ANe^{-c(\log N)^{2}}.

Therefore, (
D.13
) follows from taking expectations of both sides (and again using the facts that
|H(E)|\leq A
and
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE=\pi
for all
E\in\mathbb{R}
).

Averaging (
D.13
) over
\mathfrak{K}=n^{\prime-1}\cdot\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}
disjoint intervals
\llbracket i_{1},i_{2}\rrbracket
covering
\llbracket\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, we deduce

(D.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{K}}\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]\Bigg|\leq A.\end{aligned}

By
Lemma

D.3
, taking the expectation of (
D.5
), and using the fact that
|H(\lambda)|\leq A
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
(and also the fact that
2\mathfrak{K}\cdot N\geq 2\mathfrak{K}\cdot n^{\prime}\geq 2\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}\geq\mathfrak{N}
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}^{\prime}}^{n_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary G_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]\Bigg|\leq 7A(\log N)^{5};

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}H(\nu)\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{K}\pi}\displaystyle\int_{-\log N}^{\log N}H(E)\displaystyle\sum_{i=\mathfrak{N}_{1}^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{N}_{2}^{\prime}}\Imaginary\mathfrak{G}_{ii}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)dE\bigg]\Bigg|\leq 15A(\log N)^{5}.

Together with (
D.14
), this yields

(D.15)

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\bigg]-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}H(\nu)\bigg]\Bigg|\leq 23A(\log N)^{5}.

Further multiplying the first bound in (
D.1
) by
\mathfrak{N}\mathfrak{K}^{-1}\leq 2N
; using the bound

\displaystyle\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{N}}{\mathfrak{K}}-n\bigg|=n\cdot\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{N}}{\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}}\cdot\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}-1\bigg|\leq n\cdot\big|\big((1+3n^{-1}(\log N)^{5}\big)^{2}-1\big|\leq 7(\log N)^{5}

as
n-2(\log N)^{5}\leq n^{\prime}\leq n
and
\mathfrak{N}-2(\log\mathfrak{N})^{5}\leq\mathfrak{N}^{\prime}\leq\mathfrak{N}
; using the fact that
|H(\lambda)|\leq A
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
; and taking expectations further yields

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{K}}\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\eig\bm{\mathfrak{L}}}H(\nu)\bigg]-n\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\leq 7A(\log N)^{5}+2A+2ANe^{-(\log N)^{2}}\leq 8A(\log N)^{5}.

Together with (
D.15
), this gives

\displaystyle\Bigg|\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sum_{i=n_{1}}^{n_{2}}H(\Lambda_{i})\bigg]-n\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}H(\lambda)\varrho(\lambda)d\lambda\Bigg|\leq 31A(\log N)^{5},

which yields the lemma.
∎
